# The Room Beyond the Threshold

*2016 Commonwealth causal novel — Chapter XXIII candidate. NONCANON / independent editorial HOLD. Sole limited interior viewpoint: proposed fictional Jake / CHAR-JAKE-KALOPER / PRINCIPAL_CHARACTER, one of the exclusive twelve current owner characters. This is invented secondary-world fiction, not verified ESPN history or an authenticated historical owner-slot assignment. The shelter, market, west store, workers, law, economy and all dialogue remain provisional pending period-correct Character Canon and Atlas review. The protected 2026 Prologue and Chapters I–III are not revised. No verified competitive result is asserted.*

### I. The price of speaking clearly

The notice had already been nailed to the board when Jake arrived.

For a moment he was so astonished to see it there that he stopped in the lane and allowed a cart to pass on the wrong side of him. Its driver called something about choosing a place to stand. Jake stepped into the mud without answering. The notice was beneath the edge of the weigh-stones awning, where he had failed to put it the day before. A dull new nail held each top corner. The lower edge lifted a little in the wind.

Esi stood beside the board with Tern and the keeper of the stones. She had brought a small stool to reach the upper rail, though it was Tern who had done the nailing. There was another sheet below the notice recording the fee and the hour of posting. The men who had been trying to sell black-circle papers the previous day were nowhere Jake could see. There were plenty of people who had not sold them and who now seemed to know more about the theft than he did.

"You said we would pay it in the morning," he told Esi.

"We did."

"I meant I would come."

"You were expected at the serving door. The sacks came early."

He had unloaded two of them before discovering the list of deliveries under his coat. He had been glad to do something that needed no interpretation. Now he looked at the notice and felt his relief curdle into the wish that the work had waited for him.

Esi read his face without asking him to improve it. "I was not going to leave the public board empty so you could have the satisfaction of filling it."

"I know."

"Good."

Tern pressed the notice's lower edge flat. The words were plain. The shelter's incoming-supplies account book had been removed without permission. No payment, obligation, room assignment, claimant, or missing person could be certified from papers appearing elsewhere in its name. Those with an existing matter should come to the shelter with their own surviving evidence. Nothing was offered in exchange for a black circle.

The market did not become more orderly because the words were true. A woman reading over Tern's shoulder asked whether the shelter could guarantee that names had not escaped. Another man wanted to know whether a debtor could use the announcement to avoid payment. The keeper of the stones interrupted them both to say that a posted notice was not a court and the board had no power to make people believe it.

"Can you guarantee no one has copied the book?" the woman asked again.

Esi said, "No."

There was an audible dissatisfaction around that one syllable. Jake understood it. He had wanted the announcement to stop a thing moving through the world. Esi had told the truth about its movement, which was much less comforting.

A porter came up from the west store. Jake recognized the lime on his coat but not the porter himself until the man spoke. He had seen loose pages carried in cloth; he had been careful not to name their owner. Now he held a folded square of linen at arm's length.

"I found this near the drain. It may not be yours."

Inside was a thin paper strip, torn across a line of writing. The ink had run along one edge. Jake could make out the tail of a name, a quantity of bedding, and a mark of payment whose shape might have been any number of things. There was no visible heading, no page number, no stitch hole. Anyone who had watched the shelter work could have written the words.

The woman at the board leaned closer. "That looks like a name."

"It looks like part of one," Tern said.

"And the bedding?"

Esi took one step between the paper and the crowd. "The strip will go with the report. It is not an invitation to read what remains of someone's private account."

"You just told us anyone with evidence should bring it."

"I did not tell you you could acquire another person's business by standing close enough to a hand."

The porter opened his mouth as though he had meant only to help and had discovered help could suddenly carry a price. Jake took the linen from him at Esi's request. The damp corner clung to his thumb. He felt foolishly careful of a fragment that might not be part of the stolen book at all.

Across the lane a small girl was trying to pull her brother away from a stall of wooden bowls. The boy had caught sight of the crowd and wanted to know why people were looking at paper instead of breakfast. The girl told him that if they looked longer they might get breakfast without paying. She spoke with the confidence of someone inventing a law.

Jake remembered the children who had named Mara's departure mark a road or a bed. It seemed the children could invent a nation in the space adults took to argue over a receipt.

He walked back toward the shelter carrying the strip in both hands. The notice remained behind him, properly fixed at last. It had not brought the missing pages home.

### II. A name made into a road

A woman Jake had seen for several evenings was waiting at the western gate with a rolled blanket and a pair of shoes tied together by their laces. Her name was Sola. She had been repairing kitchen mats for a meal and a dry place, an arrangement made before Mara left and continued by Esi without anyone pretending it was employment in the mill. Jake knew only what he had seen: she worked in short bursts, kept scraps of reed beneath her sleeping shelf, and carried a needle in a horn case whose lid had a crack.

Today the horn case was tied inside her blanket.

"You've come to collect the shoes?" he asked.

"No. They're mine."

"Are you going out?"

"I am standing out."

He looked from her packed blanket to the gate. "Has Esi spoken to you?"

"She has. So have three people who did not know me yesterday."

She showed him a square of paper, larger than the strip the porter had brought. A stranger had passed it beneath the outer wall. It bore her name in one line and, below it, two numbers separated by an uncertain mark. Beside them someone had written: THE ROOM IS PAID THROUGH THE HOLDER. There was no seal, no signature, no proof that the account came from the stolen book. There was only the new fact that a stranger knew how to reach her.

"Does it say what I owe?" she asked.

"I don't know what those numbers mean."

"Neither do I. But the people who carried it knew how to ask me where I slept."

He tried to find a sentence that did not turn her fear into an argument about authentication. "We can say it's false."

"We can say we do not know if it is false." She folded the paper on its old crease. "I understand the difference. I have heard it explained at this door all week."

Her irritation entered him more quickly than her meaning. Everyone seemed to know the shelter's weaknesses, and everyone seemed to know how he ought to answer them. He held the damp strip higher, meaning to tell her they had found another piece. Then he lowered it. A second uncertain scrap was not shelter.

"Someone may have watched you come and go," he said.

"That is why I am going. I don't need to know how they found out to know they have."

Sola had not asked his permission. He saw how close he had come to speaking as if she had.

She pointed toward the covered room. "There are two mats I have not finished. I will not take them. My needlework isn't a pledge against the rest of my life."

"Of course not."

"You say that because you have not been asked to repay a debt with a person."

The words struck him hard, though he could not tell what history supported them. He wanted to ask. The paper in her hand made it clear that asking was another way to make her account public.

When Esi came out, Sola spoke first. "I have told him. I'm leaving before the second meal. I will collect what I earned and no more."

Esi went into the kitchen and returned with a wrapped portion of bread and a small piece of coin that Jake had not seen her count. She named the remaining mat work, including the two pieces unfinished, and told Sola she was paying only for what had been done. Sola checked the coin. She took the bread but did not eat it.

"Do you want a witness?" Esi asked.

"I want to get down the road before anyone decides my name is a way to find me."

Tern was approaching from the lane with a thin bundle of new cord for the replacement book. Esi asked her to remain by the gate until the departure was finished. Tern nodded, though the interruption took her away from paid work.

Jake wanted to offer Sola his carrying wage. He still had a portion of it after the public-board fee had been met from the shelter's funds. He also owed money inside these walls. If he offered the coin, it might sound like he had purchased an answer to the question he had not asked. If he did not, he would watch a woman walk away with too little and tell himself restraint was virtue.

"I can carry the blanket to the turning," he said.

Sola adjusted the roll against her shoulder. "The blanket isn't heavy."

"The road is uneven."

"I can see it."

He stepped aside.

A neighbor had heard the exchange. She came with a small pair of wooden clogs that had been left beneath the outer shelf. They belonged to Sola, who had forgotten them when she packed. The neighbor knelt to tie them to the blanket and asked only whether the cord would hold. There was no ceremony to the kindness. Sola let her do it.

Jake had watched people leave the shelter before. The bread-maker had gone; Mara had gone with Senn; he himself had gone upriver and returned. He had been able to tell a story about each departure, usually one in which a road promised that a person might come back changed. Sola was not making a journey for the sake of such a story. She was removing herself from a place that had become unsafe to inhabit, and she had judged its safety without waiting for him to understand.

At the turning she looked back once. Not at Jake. At the neighbor who had tied her clogs.

### III. What a wall would cost

By noon the western room was crowded with people who had never before asked to sit in its meetings. The shelter had no rule for assemblies of this size. Mara had sometimes gathered those affected by a delivery or a shortage, but she had done it around a task. Now a mother whose children slept near the kitchen wanted the names on their bedding erased. A carpenter who used the rear bench wanted the outer gate shut at dark. The cook wanted to know who would carry water if the gate closed; the trough was outside.

Esi stood where the receipts were usually counted. She had not asked them to elect her. She had the keys, the surviving records, and the job of telling people which promises were unproved. Neither gave her the right to make the room smaller than it was.

"One at a time," she said, and at once three people began.

The mother took her children to the western wall. A board of wood had been nailed there to protect the plaster from carrying poles. She turned it around. On the back someone had scratched a mark beside the height of each child, adding a line when one grew. She did not want the marks rubbed away. She wanted them behind a shutter so strangers could not see who lived here.

The carpenter said he could fit a shutter in a day if someone supplied hinges. Tern said a shutter would hide the room without stopping the people who already knew how to find it.

"Then we close the gate," the carpenter repeated.

Len, who had been seated near the window with his unfinished bird, put down his knife. His injured eye was still covered by the thin cloth he wore when the light troubled him. He spoke from where he sat, not loudly.

"Close it and the man who comes after dark will have to knock."

"That is the point," the carpenter said.

"And someone will have to open."

"That is also the point."

"Then who stands there when he knocks?"

No one answered immediately. The rear room had not acquired a watch because nobody could agree who had the spare hours to keep one. A locked gate needed someone willing to hear knocking without knowing whether a voice belonged to a friend.

Jake looked at Len's wooden bird. Its narrow wing had become an unmistakable choice. Len was carving it as it had emerged, not trying to make the two sides equal. Jake had seen that kind of patience in a craftsperson and had envied it; his own hands tended to correct a thing before knowing whether correction was wanted.

A man by the kitchen proposed moving the sleeping shelves into the old east room. Esi shook her head before he finished.

"The east boundary is unsafe. The chest is still there behind the closed section. We do not open it to solve a different problem."

"Then we have nowhere to put them."

"We have this room. The question is how to use it."

Jake could have reminded them of the false notices and the stolen ledger and the suspected cover in the west store. He thought of laying the new strip on the table, letting them see that another uncertain piece had come back. The thought pleased the part of him that wanted the room to understand he had not been idle. Then the mother asked whether her children's sleeping places were recorded in the stolen book, and Jake realized that he had not even asked which of its pages might have listed them.

"We don't know," he said.

"You don't know?" she repeated.

"No."

"Then why are we here listening to what you found?"

He had no answer that could ease her. He wanted to say that he was listening too. It would have sounded like a claim to equal injury. The people who had slept in the room while he was away had lived through changes made without him; now the loss of their information reached beyond these walls. The stolen book was not an adventure assigned to him by the shelter. It belonged to the people whose names had been kept in it.

A young worker who carried coal for the kitchen said the worst danger was not a person coming inside, but a person deciding never to come again. He pointed to Sola's empty shelf. "She wasn't afraid of a gate. She was afraid of someone waiting beyond it."

Tern set down her cord. "You can hang a curtain," she said. "You can close a door. Neither makes a copied name uncopy itself."

"Have you got something better?" the carpenter asked.

She did not pretend she had. She said she could make a portable screen with wood frames and cloth if the materials were paid for, but that a screen was no answer to the stolen information. She was prepared to quote a price, not give the room a cure.

The mother asked for the screen anyway. Not because she trusted it to protect her children from strangers, she said, but because children should be able to sleep without anyone looking in from the lane. Others agreed; a few objected that the material should buy bread. Esi wrote the expense beside the planned binding of the new book. She called it a proposal until the cook supplied the remaining kitchen count.

Then she asked the one question nobody had expected: "Who among you wants to keep sleeping here if the gate stays open?"

Hands rose slowly. Some did not rise at all.

Jake looked at his own. He had not moved it. His old place had been given to an injured newcomer; he slept wherever a board could be found. He had returned believing this was still his place in some unspoken way, and he could not decide whether a hand raised now would express his need or steal a share of someone else's fear.

Len raised his hand only halfway. "I want a gate that opens," he said. "And someone there who knows how to hear."

### IV. The room no ledger held

The supper bell brought no resolution. It brought food, which was a kind of temporary discipline. The cook made everyone put the debate aside long enough to pass bowls without dropping them. She had taken the three newly delivered sacks into her count and divided them carefully. The meal did not prove the older supplier's claim had been paid. It proved that grain could still become bread while an argument remained alive.

Jake took his bowl to the yard.

The light had gone flat over the roofs, and carts passing outside sounded farther away than they were. He could see Sola's empty shelf through the open side door. No one had removed its reed scraps. Someone had put her unfinished mat flat beneath a board so it would not curl. The sight troubled him more than the paper bearing her name. There were marks of labor here that had never been placed in any book.

Tern came out with a bowl of her own. She remained standing.

"You asked earlier whether you could carry the blanket," she said. "You could have offered to carry it without making her answer another question."

"I did offer."

"She said no."

"Yes."

"Then you listened. It is not a crime to let a person refuse you."

He would have liked that to be the end of it, a verdict in which doing nothing had become the correct act. Tern kept watching the yard.

"She asked me for work last week," Tern said. "At my shop."

Jake turned. "Did you take her?"

"No. I told her the binding job was not ready and that I could not promise wages. That was true. It still means she'll go somewhere else now."

"Could you send word?"

"To where?"

He looked down at the bowl. The question had been waiting for him all afternoon in different mouths. If he could not name a place to send word, he could not pretend to promise a reply.

Tern said she would finish the new binding if Esi paid the deposit already discussed. She would not take the suspected original cover out of the west store until the measure-keeper released it under the agreed hold; and she would not describe a torn strip as genuine merely because it resembled an account. Her trade had limits. She meant to keep them without leaving the shelter to manage alone.

"Are you angry with me?" Jake asked.

"About which thing?"

He almost laughed. "The notice. The book. Whatever you have heard I was supposed to do."

"I am tired of my name being used as the person who knows how to fix everything with paper." She looked toward the open doorway, where the mother was folding the bedding against the inner wall. "I would rather make a screen that lets her children sleep. It will take two days. It won't turn a stranger away. But it is work I can finish."

That was a more substantial answer than the one he had been seeking. Jake put his empty bowl on the outer bench. A shutter, a screen, a person staying at the gate: none could recover a stolen page. They could change the shape of a night that had not yet arrived.

When he returned indoors, Esi was laying a smaller slate on the table. Instead of making a second record of who occupied which shelf, she had drawn three columns: night water, gate watch, and kitchen fire. A woman who could carry water had put down two evenings; the coal worker offered one night of watch so long as someone relieved him at dawn. The carpenter offered the first frame for Tern's screen if his paid bench work allowed it. No one could promise more than they had.

Jake knew he could offer to stand at the gate. He also knew his seasonal upriver offer could not remain unanswered indefinitely. The thought of leaving while the shelter was vulnerable made him ashamed; the thought of staying only so he could feel indispensable made him angrier still.

Esi saw him standing with his hand near the slate. "What can you actually do?"

"I can stand the first watch."

"You have the serving door at dawn."

"I can take the later half if someone wakes me."

"That is not the first watch."

He closed his mouth. Then he said, "I can stand the later half."

She wrote his name in that space, with a question mark beside the relief time until the coal worker agreed. It was the smallest mark on the slate. He found himself looking at it as if it had been carved in stone.

Len came to the table and asked whether anyone would object if he sat near the watch door. He could listen for carts, he said; his eye troubled him less when he was not staring at a flame. The carpenter protested that a man with a bad eye should not be made to guard a gate.

"I asked to sit," Len said. "I did not ask to be made."

Esi offered him a place beside the inner latch, with no responsibility for deciding whom to admit. Len agreed to that and no more. He carried his unfinished bird with him.

They had not voted a new law into being. They had worked out how to get through one night with people who could refuse, argue, and change their minds. Jake was disappointed by how fragile the arrangement looked. He was also reluctant to call it small.

### V. The road that had no witness

The first knocking came before the evening watch had properly begun.

Jake was carrying water to the serving room when the sound reached him. Three quick blows, a silence, and one more. The coal worker was taking his place by the gate. Len had settled on the inside stool, his bird in one hand, the other resting on the bench where he could feel the knocks pass through the wood.

"Who is there?" the coal worker called.

A voice answered that a woman had left a bundle beneath the west wall and someone had been sent to fetch it. The speaker did not say the woman's name. He asked for the gate to be opened.

Jake could see the coal worker look to Esi. She had brought the surviving receipts into the covered room; she came without them, wearing the keys inside her coat.

"Which bundle?" she asked through the gate.

"The one tied with brown cord."

"There are several kinds of cord," she said.

The man beyond the gate swore softly. "I was told the keeper could identify it."

"The keeper is away."

"Then send the one who holds the keys."

"I am here."

A pause. Jake imagined the man thinking of how much the phrase might be worth if carried back to the market. He caught himself turning the pause into motive. Through a gate a person could be frightened, impatient, or simply trying to find a package. He had no right to choose which.

The man said a woman called Sola had left a roll for a relative. No one inside knew of such a roll. Jake knew she had departed with her blanket and shoes; he did not know whether she had made arrangements elsewhere. The horn needle case, the damp lane, the neighbor tying clogs: all those things lived in his memory, not in a transaction he could defend.

Esi told the man they would not release goods without identifying the property and the person authorized to receive it. She did not accuse him of lying. She told him where the public notice could be read. When the man demanded that she come outside and look at a token, she refused to move the keys from their cord.

Then a small object slid beneath the gate.

It was not a token. It was a wooden clog, its strap torn loose, with the end of a binding cord still knotted through the heel.

Jake recognized the shape. The neighbor had tied two clogs to Sola's blanket that morning. He could not know whether this was one of them. There were many clogs with worn heels in the market. But the cord had been knotted in a hurry, and his stomach contracted before he could stop it.

"Where did you get that?" he asked.

"From the turning," the voice replied. "I found it in the road. I was told the woman who dropped it came from here."

"Who told you?"

"Does it matter? I am trying to give back a shoe."

It did matter. It also did not prove the man had done anything wrong. Jake could hear the difference between his fear and the evidence. His fear was telling him to open the gate and follow the stranger, through the lane, past the weigh-stones, and perhaps farther, until the road gave up a woman he had allowed to walk away.

Len spoke from his stool. "You can leave it there."

The coal worker looked down. The clog lay inside the threshold but out of reach unless someone bent.

"We can keep the shoe safe," Len said. "You don't need the gate open to return a shoe."

"Are you saying I cannot come inside?" the stranger called.

"No," Esi answered. "I am saying this gate is closed for the night. In the morning a person may come with a name and a request. If the shoe belongs to someone here, we'll find a way to return it. We are not taking responsibility for a story about the person who wore it."

The stranger stood outside long enough for Jake to hear his breathing through the gap. Then his steps retreated. They did not hurry. That made Jake more uneasy than flight would have. He had been ready to chase a villain. A person departing at an ordinary pace left him nothing to do with his anger.

The coal worker secured the inner latch. He lifted the clog and placed it on a narrow shelf where Esi could examine it in daylight. The act was so modest that Jake almost objected. A woman might have lost her shoe; someone might have carried it back; someone else might have used it to learn whether the gate could be opened with a story. Those possibilities could not be collapsed into one answer, and none told him where Sola had gone.

Later, on the half-watch he had actually promised, he sat near Len and listened to the road. He heard two carts, a loose wheel striking a stone, and a singer whose tune he knew but whose words did not carry. He heard no further knocking.

"Do you think it was hers?" he asked Len.

"I don't know."

"You saw the cord."

"I heard you say there was cord."

Jake remembered the injured eye and the years Len had spent learning which things he could trust without looking at them. He wondered if Len might be embarrassed by so many people treating sight as the only honest witness. He did not know. Len continued turning the bird between his fingers, feeling the narrow wing.

A little before dawn the kitchen fire was stirred. The mother and her children were sleeping behind a hanging cloth, not yet inside the proposed screen. The gate remained shut. The new book was still unbound on Esi's table; the suspected old cover remained in the west store under a paid and expiring hold; the leaves and their carrier remained unknown. The old supplier's claim had not changed. Sola's shelf was still empty.

Jake heard the coal worker rise to relieve him. He did not tell the man he had solved anything. He gave him a plain account of the road, the knocks, the object placed on the shelf, and the absence of further visitors. That was the useful part.

Then he went to the serving door. He would have preferred to carry a dramatic truth with him. Instead he carried a pail that was full to the rim, taking care not to spill it before the cook could use it.

In the western room the first child woke and asked whether the new screen had arrived. Her mother said it had not been made yet.

The child accepted that answer more easily than any adult Jake had spoken to in two days. She wanted to know what it would look like. Her mother drew a square in the dust with a finger and left one side open.

Jake looked at the gap. He could not have said whether it was the beginning of a door or the space a door was meant to guard. He could say only that the child had moved nearer to her mother to see it.

He went on carrying the water.

---

### Editorial handoff — outside the fiction

- **Chapter function / causal turn:** The previously unposted theft notice is now actually posted by Esi and Tern, without rewriting Chapter XXII or claiming that it solves the missing-ledger problem. A purported private entry reaches Sola, a shelter resident who makes her own decision to leave. The collective consequences shift from procedural evidence to trust, privacy, voluntary departure and night access. Residents debate the risk of a locked gate and establish only a one-night voluntary watch arrangement, not an enduring new government or approved physical modification. A purported lost clog arrives from an unidentified stranger; it is not authenticated as Sola's, proof of injury, or evidence of a culprit.
- **POV rule:** Sole limited principal POV is fictional CHAR-JAKE-KALOPER / PRINCIPAL_CHARACTER. Esi, Sola, Tern, Len, the mother, the carpenter, coal worker and stranger are not interior narrators, and their independence is conveyed through visible choices and speech. Historical 2016 owner-slot attribution remains unverified.
- **Continuity retained:** Esi retains keys and surviving records; proposed new account book is unbound; likely old cover remains at west store under time-limited paid custody, with missing pages and culprit unknown. The old supplier obligation and Jake's partial-pledge remainder remain unresolved. Mara remains away with Senn. Len's injured eye remains impaired; the bird is still unfinished. The unsafe east-room boundary stays closed, with the other woman's chest unretrieved and distinct from the unidentified missing claimant and separate unopened message. Outstanding mill/commission, bridge/cart, singer refunds, courier note and upriver seasonal offer are not falsely settled.
- **Source separation:** All invented people, costumes, shelter interior layouts, gate watch, market fee, printing, Sola's departure, written threats, object encounter, travel and economics are NONCANON fictional proposals subject to period Character Canon and Atlas. No ESPN matches, owner identities, trades, scores, champions or other historical facts have been invented.
- **Independent review challenges:** Does the repeated language of careful evidence still dominate the fiction despite Sola's departure and collective night-watch conflict? Is Sola an independent developed character or an expendable instrument of Jake's education? Is the overnight guard arrangement socially credible, and are the gate, east-room boundary and work schedules physically coherent with prior chapters? Does the stranger with the clog create an unearned cliffhanger? Did the chapter make enough room for relationships, grief and humor? Neither self-criticism nor any passing test constitutes independent Authors Council, Character Canon, Atlas or Umpire acceptance.
- **Release state:** NONCANON / INDEPENDENT EDITORIAL HOLD. Founder-only canon promotion, merge and publication authority not invoked. Protected 2026 Prologue and Chapters I–III untouched.

# The Things She Came Back For

*2016 Commonwealth causal novel — Chapter XXIV candidate. NONCANON / independent editorial HOLD. Sole limited interior viewpoint: proposed fictional Jake / CHAR-JAKE-KALOPER / PRINCIPAL_CHARACTER, one of the exclusive twelve. Sola, Esi, Tern, Len, Deren and other inhabitants are externally observed, not new principal narrators. All material economy, invented encounters, local travel, building arrangements, proposed events and dialogue are secondary-world fiction, not verified 2016 ESPN results or authenticated owner identities. The protected 2026 Prologue and Chapters I–III remain outside this draft. Period Character Canon and Atlas clearance are not claimed.*

### I. The person who returned for work

The carpenter brought the wrong hinges.

They were sound hinges, and that was the trouble. He had found them on an old chest lid in the yard and cleaned them until they moved with a generous swing. He set them on the table beside Esi's unbound replacement ledger as if their weight might settle the matter. The mother of the two children put her finger through one of the loops.

"This will open all the way."

"It will," the carpenter said.

"Then it will open into the beds."

"We can move the beds."

"We moved them when the eastern room closed."

"We could move the table."

"The table is where the meals are counted."

The cook, who had come in to fetch a pot, said the table was where her meals were served, and she would thank everyone not to make it into a door. Nobody laughed until she went out. Then the carpenter gave the hinge a turn and admitted that the screen was not a door anyway.

Jake had finished the serving work before the sun reached the western lintel. His hands had the clean, bruised feeling of having lifted full vessels for hours. He had slept only a little during the watch, yet his body would have preferred another shift of carrying to the conversation now taking place. There was no danger he could stop by planting his feet, no long load to move with one coordinated shout. The screen, not yet made, had become a dispute about how the room ought to live.

On the gate shelf the single wooden clog lay on a piece of linen, just where the coal worker had put it. Its torn strap hung over the edge. Len had asked twice whether the shelf was in the sun, because wet wood sometimes split if it dried too quickly. The carpenter answered that the shelf was shaded. He had also offered to mend the strap, and Esi had told him to leave the thing alone until anyone who owned it could identify it. The carpenter was not pleased to have his skill withheld from a small task he could actually finish.

Jake kept looking at the clog.

He had heard the stranger's retreating footsteps. He had imagined the road beyond the turning so often in the hours before dawn that, when the morning wagons came, he half expected to find the road altered by his looking. Instead there were the same wheel ruts, the same low patch that held water after every washing, and the same porter who cursed whenever his sack caught the gate latch. For the first time since the object slid under the door, Jake was ashamed of how much of his fear he had placed in a piece of wood.

The knocking came at midmorning. Three blows, impatient and hard enough to disturb the loose dust on the lintel.

The carpenter reached for his hinges. Esi reached for the latch.

Through the gap someone said, "If you've decided to make a prison of this place, tell me and I'll go to the market."

It was Sola.

Jake felt relief so suddenly that he gripped the edge of the serving table. Relief was followed by a different unease: he had thought about her as though she belonged to the hours he had watched, a figure beyond a gate. Now her voice, irritable and entirely alive, had entered the middle of an argument about hinges.

Esi opened the gate for daylight business, not as though she were restoring anyone to the household. Sola stood outside wearing the shoes she had carried away the previous day. There was a small coil of reed under one arm and a broad strip of unfinished matting over the other. She had brought no blanket.

"You're early," Esi said.

"For which appointment?"

"We made none."

"Then I'm not early."

The cook called from within that if Sola had come to be fed she could have bread when the next batch was done. Sola answered that she had eaten and wanted a place to lay down the matting so it would not curl. She did not ask for a bed.

The mother came closer, her children behind her knees. "We thought you were gone."

"I was. I still am."

That made the children laugh. They were at an age when the possibility of being somewhere and not belonging there was more interesting than alarming. Sola lowered the reed onto the outside bench and drew a shallow breath.

"I heard there's to be a screen. Tern told a girl at the lower stall, and the girl told me because I was trying to buy fresh reeds. If the work is paid, I can give you a better panel than cloth tied to four sticks. If it isn't paid, I will finish the mats I started and go about my business."

Jake looked toward Tern, who had been speaking with the carpenter by the table. She raised both hands.

"I said a screen had been proposed. I did not hire anybody."

"I didn't say you did," Sola replied.

"You were told?"

"I was told there might be work. Now I'm asking the people who want it."

Esi fetched the smaller slate, the one that held the watch shifts. She turned it over to a clean side. Sola put down the matting and took the reed coil apart, showing its inner pieces, which were finer and paler than the outer ones. She had not come back to be found. She had come back because her craft might command a price.

Jake wanted to tell her that the strange clog had not proven anything. He had rehearsed that sentence in the dark. Before he could speak, Sola glanced at the shelf.

"That thing," she said. "Is it supposed to be mine?"

"No one knows," he answered.

"I do."

She took a pair of wooden clogs from the cloth bag at her hip. Both were worn at the inner heel, and both had the neighbor's binding cord tied through their loops. She laid them beside the returned object. The stranger's clog was longer and narrower. Its strap had been repaired with a black seam unlike either of hers.

"I took mine off the blanket before I crossed the market," she said. "A boy offered to mend one, but I had nothing to pay him with. So I carried them."

The room went very quiet. Esi looked at the three objects without touching them.

"Then that one is not yours," she said.

"It is not. You can stop telling a story in which it is."

The carpenter muttered that it had not been a very convincing story to begin with. Sola turned toward him.

"It was convincing enough that people were sent looking."

Jake had not gone looking. But his whole night's attention had been a search that only he could see. He said nothing. Sola gathered her clogs again, left the other one on the shelf, and reached for the reeds.

"Now," she said, "is there money for a screen?"

### II. An opening one could stand behind

Tern fetched her measuring cord.

It was not her best cord. The best was kept at the shop where she ruled paper and trimmed the covers of books; this one had knots at intervals and a section stained by dye. She offered it to the mother, who measured the space between the sleeping shelves and the cooking table. The children measured one another, then the carpenter's knees, then the width of the doorway. When they measured Jake he was bending to move a stack of bowls, and the smaller child announced that he had two heights.

Sola watched long enough to ask whether anyone wished to build the screen or merely to be comforted by measuring it. The mother set down the cord.

"I want a place where my children can change their clothes without the people in the lane looking straight through."

"Then a wall of reeds won't do it unless there's cloth behind," Sola said. "And if the cloth is tied tight, the first person who catches it with a pan will pull the whole frame over. Make it stand by itself. Make it bend, not fall."

The carpenter objected that a bending frame was a poor frame.

"A reed that won't bend is dry enough to break," Sola answered. "A frame that can't give ought to be bolted to a wall. You don't want to bolt it here."

The carpenter took the measure again. Then, to Jake's surprise, he put the hinges back in his box. He had expected to be asked for a pair of doors; what the mother wanted was a folding partition, which would have to be narrow enough to clear the serving table and stable enough for a child to bump it without becoming trapped beneath it.

Tern sketched three shallow leaves in chalk on a plank. Sola crossed out two of them and drew a simple woven band below the upper rails.

"If you make four leaves this broad, no one will carry them. Two can be moved by one person. The third stays against the wall, if the wall is sound."

"The western wall is sound," the carpenter said.

"You said the eastern wall was sound last year," the cook called from the kitchen.

Nobody laughed this time.

The carpenter laid his palm against the western plaster, then removed it. "I'll ask the inspector whether any fixing is permitted. We can build the leaves without fastening them today." He looked at Esi. "I am not giving up a day of paid bench work to stand here while everybody considers whether to trust a wall."

"I wouldn't ask you to," Esi said.

"That's what people say just before they ask."

There was a small, abrupt silence. Jake saw the mother look down at her children. He had heard similar silences at the mill, where a craftsman's refusal to work for goodwill alone could make him seem cruel to someone needing shelter. He understood why people made that judgment. He also remembered the daughter refusing to certify timber her father wanted to sell.

Sola spread three lengths of reed across the bench. The pale inner pieces lay among the darker ones like threads that had found an old weaving and were not certain they belonged.

"This is my price," she said. She named an amount for cutting, plaiting and fitting the panels, plus payment for material she had brought. She would work one morning and return once to finish. She would not sleep in the shelter, would not take payment in a promise of future lodging, and would be paid for the panel even if the room later chose not to use it.

The mother looked at Esi. "We don't have that much."

"We have part of it," Esi said. "But that's the money set aside for new binding."

Tern folded her chalked plan. "Don't take binding money without telling me. I've already cut the covering boards."

"I haven't taken it," Esi replied. "I'm telling you there is not enough in both places."

Jake had spent so much of the previous day thinking about the missing ledger that he had forgotten what the new one cost. It was a collection of loose leaves held beneath a board; binding did not bring back the lost pages, but it kept the surviving replacements from scattering. A screen did not recover anyone's privacy, but it made a portion of sleep less exposed. Esi could buy neither out of a wish that somebody else would work unpaid.

The children were still. The youngest had a piece of reed in one hand. Sola took it away, not sharply, and showed the child how its freshly cut end could split the skin.

"Ask before you handle a craftsperson's materials," she said.

"Can I learn?"

"Not before I have been hired."

The answer brought a gasp from the older child, as though Sola had denied the existence of learning. Then the mother laughed. It was the first unguarded laughter Jake had heard in that room since the theft. It rose and vanished without curing anything.

Esi went through the little sums on the slate. The cook, summoned to settle the question of available provisions, said the kitchen could not release its reserve. The carpenter offered to reduce the price of his frame if he could use the benches after supper, when his other work was finished. Tern would hold the cut boards another day without charging for the delay, but could not take an indefinite loss. The mother offered to sew the cloth lining herself. Two other residents who used the sleeping corner offered small coins. None of the offers was large.

Jake was tempted to put down his remaining upriver wage. He had done that before, or something near enough: offered himself as the missing piece, found too late that people needed the terms more than the gesture. This time he spoke to the mother, not Esi.

"If you accept another hand on the frame, I can help carry it after my serving work. I can't pay the whole cost."

"We need a price for the work," she said. "Not a man who feels badly about it."

He took the correction more easily than he once would have. What unsettled him was that she had said it without looking angry.

The carpenter named the labor he could accept. Sola named the reeds she could deliver. Esi put figures beside them. The sum still fell short. It was Sola who suggested the difference could be made by weaving a plain first panel now and leaving the second until another payment had been found. The mother agreed, though she had wanted a screen large enough to hide both beds.

The younger child pointed at the gap the incomplete screen would leave.

"Someone could still see me."

"Yes," her mother said.

"Then why are we buying it?"

"Because they will see less," Sola said. "And because when we can pay for more, there will be room to add it."

She set her hands on the loose reeds and waited for Esi to decide whether work was actually being offered. Esi made a deposit from the coins already placed on the table and asked Tern to mark the amount in the new loose-leaf accounts. Sola counted it herself. Only then did she sit down to work.

The scraping of reeds filled the room, a dry, clean noise unlike the turn of a mill or the swing of a hammer. Jake found that he could hear separate rhythms in it: Sola's short pull, Tern's measured cutting of linen, the carpenter setting his tools in a row. For once none of the sounds needed him to give a signal.

### III. A place outside the bargain

At the second meal a man came looking for Jake.

He was broad across the shoulders, with the hard-grained palms of the stoneworks crew, and carried a long piece of iron wrapped in coarse cloth. Jake knew him from the upriver passage, though they had exchanged few words when the frames were moved. The man had been one of the people who knew what to do when an echo made a shouted order impossible.

"I heard you had come back by the lower road," he said.

"I did."

"Deren wants to know whether you'll take the season. We have a cart going up in three mornings. After that the crew is set."

Jake glanced toward Esi's table. He had spent the journey downriver hoping the offer might remain open and the days since returning hoping it might quietly close. Hearing the deadline spoken as an ordinary fact of another crew's work made his indecision feel less like a moral struggle and more like a burden he had been allowing other people to carry.

"What are the terms now?"

"The ones he gave you. Work when there is work. Wage for hours counted, lodging while you're assigned. If the kiln passage closes, you do other hauling or wait without paid hours. We don't promise the weather."

There was no grand appointment, no commander's place, no authority over the men who had already been there longer. Jake asked whether the message needed to go back with the iron.

The man looked at the wrapped bar. "The iron is a hinge pin for a machine by the quay. I came to fetch other pieces and happened to hear you were here. I'm not your courier. But if you give me an answer before I leave, I'll carry it."

It would have been easy to take the man's unwillingness as a slight. Instead Jake was struck by the relief of speaking to someone whose entire day did not revolve around him.

"Can I answer after the second meal?"

"You can. I'll be near the lower weigh-stones till the carts are loaded."

The man went outside to sit beneath the awning, where he could see the traffic and keep the iron out of the damp. He asked for water and paid for bread. The cook charged him what she charged every traveler. She had not listened to enough of their conversation to know he might be carrying Jake's future on his return.

Jake took his bowl to the back of the yard. He had thought the seasonal work would prove he could choose a life unconnected to the shelter. Yet the offer did not release his old pledge. It did not return Mara, finish the daughter's workbench, mend Len's eye, pay the singer's refunds, or bring back the ledger. Nor would refusing it do any of those things. He had carried wages home once and made a portion of his debt smaller; that was the most that had been true.

Len was on the bench near the outside wall. He had removed the cloth from his injured eye only to tie it more securely, and was cutting the underside of his wooden bird with a knife small enough to conceal in his palm.

"That crewman talked to you," Len said.

"Yes."

"About going away?"

"Yes."

Len did not turn his head. "If you go, take your own shoes."

Jake looked at him, uncertain whether the old man meant the advice to be profound.

"Why?"

"Because the ones in the sleeping room are too small."

He laughed before he could help it. Len held up the bird and turned it once against the light, searching with his better eye for an uneven shadow.

"I used to be good at leaving a place," Len said. "It was arriving without owing everyone a story that took practice."

"Did you learn?"

"Sometimes I arrived with a better story."

Jake sat beside him. The bench creaked, though the carpenter had tightened it since the autumn repair. The world around them was full of work that did not speak the language of obligation. A wheel rolled badly and a boy struck its rim with a stick; a kettle steamed at the kitchen door; a cat beneath the far wall sat utterly still until a sparrow hopped within reach, then missed it by an embarrassing distance. Len watched the bird on his knee, which was under no threat from cats.

"Do you think she'll come back?" Jake asked.

"Who?"

He had meant Mara. He could just as easily have meant Sola, or the old bread-maker who had left before them both. He realized he wanted a single answer for every person who had gone.

"I don't know," he said.

"Then I'd not build a bench on the expectation."

When Jake went inside, Sola had fitted the first band into a frame resting on trestles. A narrow young worker had been holding it steady, and the child who wanted to learn now sat a proper distance away with a blunt length of reed she had been allowed to strip. Her concentration was terrible. She worked as though every fiber concealed a secret important enough to tell the world. Sola corrected her hand position twice, then went back to her own plaiting.

Jake stood near the door until Sola noticed him.

"You look as if there's another shoe," she said.

"No."

"Then don't stand there like a warning."

He moved into the yard, where he could help the carpenter shift the trestles away from the meal traffic. Sola went on working. Her refusal to be frightened into silence was neither his achievement nor his failure. He found he could admire the steadiness of her fingers without attempting to take ownership of the courage behind them.

### IV. The terms of absence

Esi did not sit down when Jake told her about the stoneworks cart.

She had three things to do before supper. The cook had asked for the next dry-grain count, a traveler had brought a question about a receipt lost with the original ledger, and a woman had come asking whether the shelter meant to close the gate every night. Esi answered the traveler only to the extent the surviving list allowed. She told the woman that the previous night's watch had been temporary and that the next arrangement required willing people. Then she led Jake into the yard where a stack of bowls needed cleaning.

"You have an answer to give them," she said.

"Yes."

"Then give it before a man loses his day's freight waiting for you."

"I wanted to speak to you first."

"You are doing it."

He expected her to ask him to stay. He had prepared for the appeal, and, shamefully, for the satisfaction of resisting it. Esi lifted the bowls two at a time and handed them to him to rinse.

"I have a portion of the wage," he began. "The rest of the pledge—"

"Still exists. That has not changed."

"If I go upriver, I can send payments."

"You can, if you earn the money. I won't write down money you haven't earned."

"I know."

She looked at him for the first time. "Do you?"

He placed the bowls on a dry cloth. There was a ring of pale grit at the bottom of one, ground in by a child's heel when someone had used it to prop a table. He rubbed at it with his thumb until Esi took the bowl away.

"Leave it. The cook knows what can be cleaned."

He felt the answer rising in him again: he could repay them with extra labor, he could stay until the shelter was safe, he could forego the work because he had not yet proved he deserved to depart. Before he spoke, Esi said, "You were on watch last night. You were at the serving door this morning. If you leave in three mornings, I need to replace those hours. That's the problem in front of us."

"The gate—"

"Will be the gate whether you're here or not. I need a person to carry water, and I need to know whether the coal worker is willing to take another watch. The gate cannot be guarded by your guilty conscience."

It was not a gentle thing to hear. He had been so occupied with whether going would be forgiven that he had offered no useful account of what going would cost.

They crossed the yard to the smaller slate. His name remained in the later-watch space, beside a little question mark where relief had once been uncertain. Esi asked him to put down the last shift he could actually work. He did. Three mornings hence, he said, the cart would leave from the lower market. If he missed it, he had no reason to believe Deren would keep a place.

Esi wrote the date as a proposed departure, not a proven arrival. She assigned the serving work he would finish before going. The cook came out and moved one of those mornings because the meal count would be heavier; a younger porter agreed to carry the water that day if someone showed him the uneven path. Jake offered to do the showing that afternoon.

No one blessed his journey. No one forbade it. Esi asked how his existing partial payment was recorded, and he showed her the line she had written when he returned. She counted it and agreed it remained paid. The remaining pledge was still due. It was neither erased nor enlarged by his decision to work elsewhere.

"Do you want me to ask Mara?" he said.

Esi looked toward the road, though Mara's wagon had disappeared a day ago.

"Ask her what?"

"Whether I should go."

"She is away at work of her own."

He nodded, embarrassed by how readily he had tried to make her absence into an instruction.

Esi went to the table where the loose account leaves were stacked. Without opening the private claimant file, she drew a new line for Jake's intended departure and the shifts he had relinquished. The page could be changed if the cart did not come. It was an unremarkable arrangement, but he could feel something shift: not the burden, exactly, but the belief that uncertainty excused him from choosing.

In the sleeping corner the first reed panel stood upright between two trestles. It was not fastened, and no child was permitted behind it yet. The mother ran her palm along the band Sola had woven, then held a cloth up to see how the light passed through.

"There's still a seam," she told Sola.

"There will be until we put on the lining."

"Can you do it before supper?"

"No. I said tomorrow."

"We thought—"

"I told you my hours and my price."

The mother's shoulders sank. Jake expected another argument about need, money, or gratitude. Instead the mother said, "Tomorrow, then." She collected the cloth, and the two women began talking about which side should be washable and which should face the children. The bargain held even though the work was unfinished.

Jake had expected to see his place in the shelter shrink with every decision made without him. Watching Sola and the mother measure the seam, he wondered whether the place had always been larger than he was able to stand in.

### V. What remained in the room

He gave his answer at the lower weigh-stones before the carts departed.

The stoneworks man was fitting the wrapped iron beneath the lashings of a freight wagon. He was arguing with the driver about where the load should sit, not because the iron was precious but because it could strike a wheel on a rough descent. He acknowledged Jake with a lift of his chin.

"I'll take the work," Jake said. "If the cart comes in three mornings, I'll be ready."

"Be there before the weight is checked."

"Where do I stand?"

"Beside the cart. Don't stand under it. That's the first rule."

The man returned to the lashings. He had accepted the answer; he had not entered a ceremony. Jake stayed long enough to help lift a crosspiece and then stepped aside when the driver took over. There was no wage in that small assistance, and no claim that the season had begun.

The next day the frame took its proper shape.

The carpenter had worked an hour after his paid bench job and brought two light braces in the morning. Tern supplied a lining that could be taken off for washing. Sola arrived with her own reed knife and the second length of matting. She did not sleep at the shelter, and she would not tell Jake where she had slept. When a child asked whether the screen would make all the outside people disappear, Sola said, "It will let you decide whether they look at you while you're trying to change your clothes. You still have to put your shoes on to go outside."

The little girl considered this and said it seemed a poor bargain for the price. The mother replied that a child who spent the whole day wanting to be seen might not understand the luxury of choosing when to be seen.

They fitted the panel in the western corner. It had only two leaves. The third would remain an expense for some later time. When the carpenter set it upright it leaned, and he scowled as though the frame had spoken disrespectfully to him. Sola loosened the lower weave, Tern adjusted the cloth, and he moved one brace a finger's width. It stood. A child bumped it with an elbow and the nearest leaf folded rather than fell.

The mother put the children behind it to change their clothes after washing. Their voices came through clearly; their small bodies did not. The younger child called out that she could still see the floor. The older one said that floors could be trusted with secrets. It was not a noble observation, but it pleased the cook enough that she gave each child a piece of warm crust.

Sola collected the balance of her payment from Esi. She counted every piece, then set a portion aside to buy more reeds before leaving. Tern reminded her that the second leaf would need adjustment if the room ever bought a third.

"I know how to find work," Sola said.

"Do you know how to find me?" Tern asked.

"You're the only woman in the market who charges twice for a straight edge."

Tern gave an offended look so skillful that Sola laughed. The sound held no promise of return.

Jake found Sola at the gate when she was ready to leave. She had folded her tools into a narrow bundle. Her shoes were dusty and both clogs were again in her bag. She had come back twice without asking to be sheltered, and the room now contained something she had made.

"I was frightened last night," he said.

She looked past him at the unclaimed clog on the shelf. "I gathered."

"I thought someone had hurt you."

"Someone may have hurt the person who owns that shoe. Or it may have been dropped." She looked back at Jake. "Do not make me the answer to that question."

He nodded. There was more he wanted to say: that he was sorry he had not followed, sorry he had wanted to follow, sorry he had not been useful in the way he imagined usefulness. All of it placed his confusion between her and the road.

"Are you leaving again?" he asked.

"I told you I was never staying."

"Will you work with Tern?"

"If she pays for the binding she wants, and if I want the work when she asks."

She hefted the bundle onto her shoulder.

"I liked the little one," she said. "She listens badly but she knows when she's made a hole. That's something."

There was laughter in her face, and a reluctance to stand there any longer explaining herself. Jake moved aside so she could pass. This time he did not make an offer to carry what she was carrying.

At the turning Sola stopped to buy bread from a stall. She paid with some of the money earned at the shelter. The seller gave her bread, she gave him a coin, and neither looked back at the gate. Jake could see the exchange from the yard. It was ordinary, and therefore precious in a way his imagined rescue had not been.

The stranger's clog remained unidentified. The stolen book's suspected cover remained at the west store under a hold that would soon expire. The removed leaves and their carrier were unknown. The other woman's chest remained beyond an unsafe boundary; the missing claimant's private file and the separate unopened message were not touched. Mara had not returned with Senn. Len's eye had not healed. The singer was still owed refunds, the daughter's commission was unsigned, and Jake still owed a remainder of his pledge. No screen could cancel these facts.

By evening the mother had put a small lamp behind the new partition. It threw a pattern of reed shadows on the western wall, an uneven net of lines with an open place where the two leaves met. The child who had learned to strip reed tried to press her hand through the gap. Her mother told her to stop, not because the gap was dangerous, but because she wished to keep the work whole for one night.

Jake was preparing to leave for the stoneworks in two mornings now. He had finished the water path with the younger porter, shown him where the cart ruts made a vessel sway, and handed back the watch stool to the coal worker without asking for another turn. His name had not disappeared from the slate. It had been moved to the column for work completed.

Len sat near the screen's outer edge, turning the wooden bird in his hands. He said he could hear the children through the weave and liked that they were close enough to argue with one another without an audience.

"Do you suppose the whole thing will last?" Jake asked him.

"The screen?"

"The room."

Len ran his thumb over the bird's unequal wings. "I wasn't asked to make a room," he said. "I was asked to sit and listen for a knock. That I managed."

Jake looked at the half-open partition. Inside, the children were settling down. Outside, the cook was putting away her pots. Sola's work stood between them, not to keep the world away but to offer a choice within it. There was no lesson inscribed on the wood, no name of a person to thank in perpetuity.

When the last lamp was lowered, Jake went to the serving door and set out the vessels for morning. He placed the lightest one where the new porter would find it first. The worker would still have to learn the path. The stoneworks cart might still fail to arrive. Sola might never come back. There were things he could do about each tomorrow, and things he could not.

The reeds shifted once in the night breeze, and the screen held its place.

---

### Editorial handoff — outside the fiction

- **Causal turn and literary purpose:** The previous chapter's apparent lost-clog menace is deliberately deflated when Sola returns with both of her own clogs, not because her absence was a false alarm but because she is a working person independent of Jake's fear. The stranger's clog remains of unestablished ownership; this chapter does not identify the stranger, culprit or intended message. Sola bids for and completes paid reed-panel work on explicit terms, without returning as a dependent resident or accepting a rescuer. The residents acquire one usable two-leaf portable screen with a remaining unpaid third leaf, not a solved security system.
- **Continuity and labor:** Esi keeps the shelter keys and surviving loose-leaf replacement account, Tern's future binding work remains separately priced, and the carpenter supplies paid frame work without certification of the unsafe eastern boundary. Len's eye is still impaired and his unequal-wing bird unfinished. Sola's two original clogs remain hers; the unidentified third clog stays on the gate shelf. Jake accepts the previously offered seasonal upriver work through an observed intermediary and plans to meet a cart in three mornings; he does not claim to have arrived, started earning wages, discharged his partial pledge remainder, or gained authority over the shelter. Watch and serving shifts are actually reassigned.
- **Historical and Atlas boundary:** All shelter, market, stoneworks, screen fabrication, seasonal offer details, cart departure, workdays, chronology, law, prices and dialogue are proposed 2016 secondary-world fiction, pending period-specific Character Canon and Atlas validation. No provider-authenticated 2016 owner-slot association, new competitive result, fantasy trade, postseason event, division, championship, or fact about a real owner's private motives is asserted. The retained G01 2016 source archive is unchanged.
- **Other open loops:** Missing ledger leaves and culprit unknown; suspected cover still subject to expiring west-store hold; old supplier invoice disputed; missing claimant's private original and separate unopened message remain protected; other woman's chest unretrieved past closed unsafe east boundary; Mara away with Senn; mill repairs, wheelwright daughter's unsigned work, bridge/cart repairs, singer refunds, courier note and earlier obligations not resolved.
- **POV and release:** Only proposed fictional Jake / CHAR-JAKE-KALOPER has limited interior access. Sola, Esi, Tern, Len, Deren's unnamed crewman, children and working residents possess externally evident agency but are not thirteenth principal POVs. NONCANON / INDEPENDENT EDITORIAL HOLD. No Author Council acceptance, Character/Atlas approval, independent Umpire signoff, Founder-only canon authorization, merge or publication is represented.
- **Editorial self-challenge (not independent acceptance):** Does Sola's rapid return too conveniently dismiss the clog cliffhanger? Does using her to make a privacy screen still let her serve primarily Jake's education despite the paid independent bargain? Is a two-day screen installation technically plausible given the carpenter's separate paid work? Does the dialogue state the story's themes too openly? Does Jake's acceptance of the seasonal offer create a consequential future obligation or merely another deferred exit? Independent reviewers should assess these questions, period craft plausibility, and the novel's emerging repetitive pattern before acceptance.
