# The Mile Beyond the Weigh-Stones

*2016 Commonwealth causal novel — Chapter XXV candidate. NONCANON / INDEPENDENT EDITORIAL HOLD. Sole limited interior viewpoint: proposed fictional Jake / CHAR-JAKE-KALOPER / PRINCIPAL_CHARACTER, one of the exclusive twelve canonical league-owner identities. The proposed 2016 Jake point of view is NOT an authenticated historical ESPN owner-slot association. All roads, stoneworks, shelter life, dialogue, persons, fares, wagons, labor customs, weather and incidents below are invented secondary-world fiction subject to period Character Canon and Atlas approval. No new league result is asserted. Protected 2026 Prologue and Chapters I–III on main are untouched.*

### I. The things that had already moved

On the morning the cart was due, someone had taken Jake's place at the water trough before he woke.

He could hear the younger porter outside, measuring his steps with the clink of empty vessels. Twice the boy stopped to shift the carrying pole; the third time he cleared the low place in the lane without striking the gate. The scrape of the latch came next, followed by a woman's request for hot water, the cook's answer, and the small bodily sounds of a house acquiring its day. It was not yet light enough for the reed screen to cast a shadow.

Jake sat up beneath the western eave. His bundle had remained tied since the previous evening. There were two spare shirts, the hard heel of a loaf, a length of cord, and a small whetstone that Len had insisted belonged to him only until Jake bought his own. Jake had given it back. Len had returned it wrapped in paper without another explanation.

The sleeping room was quieter than before Mara left. Not empty, though. Someone coughed behind the screen; one of the children corrected the other in a whisper loud enough to be heard across the entire room. The injured newcomer who had taken Jake's old pallet slept with one foot outside his blanket. The foot trembled every few breaths, as if it were trying to walk without its owner.

Jake had expected departure to present itself as a scene. He had pictured Esi with the keys and a last instruction, Len lifting his unfinished wooden bird in farewell, the younger porter making a clumsy attempt to take the water before Jake could intervene. Instead the water had already been taken. There was a crust on his bundle because the cook had put it there when he was asleep.

At the table Esi was folding two new slips of paper. They were not copies of the missing account leaves. One named a recent delivery of flour and the witnesses who had seen it unloaded. The other recorded the fee the west store demanded to extend its hold on a brown cover which might once have belonged to the stolen account book. The hold would expire that evening.

"That is today," Jake said.

Esi set a weight on the second slip. "I can count."

"Who is going?"

"Me. And the boy, after he finishes the water."

"I could go before the cart."

"You could. But the man at the west store opens when he opens, and the cart is not waiting for either of us."

He moved the strap of his bundle along his shoulder, testing the weight. Esi's ink pot stood at the edge of the table. The loose leaves of her replacement account were pinned beneath a piece of split wood because Tern had not yet been paid to bind them. There were empty lines for goods not yet received and a clear statement of the older supplier invoice that remained disputed. Esi had written nothing beneath the line for the stolen book's missing pages.

"There may be a charge for keeping the cover," Jake said.

"There will be. I will decide whether the house can bear it after I hear the amount. I am not sending you to bargain on a price you cannot pay for us."

He reached for the nearest vessel, then stopped. The cook was standing behind him.

"Put that down. It has already been scrubbed."

"It wasn't for—"

"It never is," she said. "If you need breakfast, eat. If you need to be useful, there is a man in the yard with a broken strap. If you need to go, the road has not become shorter while you've talked."

The man with the strap had come to borrow a needle and was waiting for Tern. Jake could not mend leather with the needle he had. The cook gave him porridge anyway, and a spoon whose handle bent to one side.

Len was sitting on the outside bench with his damaged eye covered. His bird lay in his lap, one wing still longer than the other. The morning wind had become cold enough to lift his sleeve.

"You're not carving," Jake said.

"Not where I can't see the blade."

"Do you want me to put it inside?"

"Is that what you want to do with everything?"

Jake almost answered in kind. Len looked at the bundle and touched the paper around the whetstone.

"Keep the edge away from the shirts. That's advice about the stone, not about your future."

Jake took the bundle to the gate. On the shelf the stranger's unidentified clog remained on its linen, with the torn strap laid to one side. Sola had taken both of her own clogs when she left after completing the two-leaf screen. She had earned her payment; she had not agreed to return. The third leaf of the screen was still unbought, and the partition did not pretend to make the house secure.

Esi came into the lane holding the two slips.

"Your remaining pledge is here," she said, touching the corner of her ledger, not the folds in her hand. "When there is payment, there will be a receipt. If there isn't, there won't."

"I know."

"Then I won't spend your morning repeating it."

Jake watched the younger porter lift the last full vessel across the threshold. He used the smoother side of the path Jake had shown him, but he had learned on his own to bend his knees before clearing the step.

The boy did not look at Jake for permission to pass.

### II. A wheel that belonged to somebody else

The lower market was already congested when Jake arrived. The weigh-stones had been uncovered, and a queue of traders stood beneath the awning to have their freight measured before joining the long road. A broad cart with a blue patch sewn into its canvas hood waited on the street beyond. He recognized the stoneworks man's iron bar lashed along its left side.

A driver with a narrow jaw and a split thumbnail was arguing over a wooden packing block.

"It's too thin," the driver said. "Not for the axle. For the last length of iron. We take the turn with that weight sitting there, and it moves."

The person who had brought the block was the wheelwright's daughter.

She wore a plain work apron over clothes marked with old sawdust, and she held her measuring cord in a way that told Jake she had been using it since before sunrise. Her father's unfinished wheel had once occupied the attention of the entire yard, though she had refused to let his public promises stand for her measurements. She had taken work from another customer and had left her own commission unsigned rather than be made responsible for a machine she had not certified.

"The block is the thickness you requested," she said. "If your iron is moving, it wants a wedge against the cart bed, not padding laid on top. You ordered a block."

"The man who ordered it isn't here."

"Then he can pay me for this one, and you can order the other."

A few traders were watching. None had reason to know the old quarrel at the mill. The driver's problem was an ordinary one to them: a load had to move, a mistake had been made, and both sides hoped the other would absorb the cost.

Jake wanted to approach without making the matter about their last conversation. He had apologized to her upon his return, and she had accepted that he had spoken. She had not pronounced him forgiven or agreed that his intentions were relevant to her father's injured trade.

The daughter saw him before he could decide what to say.

"You've come to take the cart."

"Yes."

"Then stand where he can see you. They won't count a passenger who hides behind the load."

She went back to the driver. Jake stepped aside for a boy carrying a crate of glazed cups.

The driver pulled the iron length outward until it shifted against its rope. It was heavier toward one end. He showed the daughter where the cart bed had a shallow worn place; each time the iron settled there, the rope slackened a fraction.

She examined it from the side rather than climbing under the load. Then she took the thin block she had made and set it beneath the lighter end, not as an answer but to see the change.

"There's your problem," she said. "The two ends aren't bearing on the same plane. Put a proper chock at the heavy end and take the strain off the lashings. The wood I brought won't do that job. It wasn't meant to."

The driver pressed both hands into his eyes. He had paid someone to bring the wrong piece and did not want to pay again.

Jake could feel a familiar impulse rise: fetch a piece of wood, solve the problem cheaply, win a little standing with people who remembered him as a man who had promised too much. A stall behind him sold firewood of several lengths. He could buy a block, trim it with the whetstone if it needed trimming, and say that the cart could go.

But he knew enough of the wheelwright's daughter to understand that finding wood and certifying a load were different acts. He said nothing.

She selected a discarded wedge from beneath the weigh-stones platform. It was hard wood, worn at one edge, fit for demonstrating the shape but not fit for service. She placed it under the iron and showed how the load settled. When she withdrew it, the iron shifted back.

"That's the shape," she said. "Not the piece. I can cut you one if the stallkeeper will lend me his saw, and if you agree to the price before I start. I can't promise you the wheel won't fail. I can promise this end won't wander across the bed for lack of a chock."

The driver stared at the sky. Then he asked her price.

She named it. He made a face, counted the time he had already lost, and accepted. He paid half in advance and watched her mark the cut. It was a small transaction. Jake had seen men make speeches over smaller ones. The daughter merely took the wood to the saw.

A porter asked Jake to lift a crate while the driver moved another. He did, keeping one hand under its corner until the porter took the weight. Then he went to the daughter, who was sawing with short, economical strokes.

"I am going upriver," he said.

"I know. You came with the bundle."

"I wanted to say—"

She stopped the saw but did not look up. "If you're about to apologize again, I have a paid edge to finish."

He looked down at the clean line opening in the wood. "I was going to ask if you need anything carried to the works."

"I don't. I sell my work here. If I need a carrier, I'll hire one whose delivery I can track."

There was an answer in that which did not require him to defend himself. The noise of the market moved around them: a horse stamping, the clatter of grain measures, a laugh from the rope stall. She finished the cut and fitted the new chock without asking Jake to help.

When the driver tightened the rope again, the iron stayed where it belonged.

"Will you write your name on it?" he asked.

"On the wood?"

"On the receipt."

"I'll write what I supplied. You have my mark on the first half-payment."

The driver paid the rest. The daughter rolled her cord and walked away toward her own bench. Jake did not follow. A real piece of her work was going up the road, and she had made sure its price and its limits would travel with it. He was going by the same route. The coincidence bought him no part in her future.

### III. The road accepts the load

There was room for Jake on the backboard between two stacks of wrapped iron. The driver told him to sit with his feet inward, then told him to get down again so the cart could be weighed. Two men checked the straps, the position of the iron, and the pair of horses. The stoneworks crewman who had carried Deren's offer was nowhere in sight. Another laborer, younger and scarred across one forearm, had a list of cargo written on stiff card.

"Name?" the laborer asked.

Jake gave it.

"Passenger or contracted hand?"

"Hand. For the stoneworks season."

"That isn't a wage for the road."

"I didn't ask for one."

The laborer wrote his name beneath a short column of travelers. The driver counted the available space once more and charged a modest fare for the ride. Jake paid it from what remained of the two days' wages he had earned upriver before. The fee hurt more than he had expected. Employment had seemed to begin the instant he said he would take it; now the road was taking money from him before a single paid hour had begun.

A woman with a cloth-bound box was waiting beside the cart. She had a pale scar at the angle of her jaw and carried no more than she could lift alone. The driver explained that her box was freight and she was not booked as a passenger. She wanted to know whether she could walk alongside until the river. The driver said the road was public as far as he knew but the cart was not a shelter. She thanked him without warmth.

Jake watched her take her position near the rear wheel. Whatever was in the box was hers to carry or consign. She neither asked him to lift it nor looked at him long enough to make him an offer. He would have liked to know whether she was going to the same place as he was. Instead he climbed onto the backboard and checked that his bundle would not fall beneath a wheel.

The first movement was so slight that for an instant he thought the horses had only leaned into their collars. Then the weigh-stones began to slip backward. The stallkeeper raised a hand to the driver, and the driver answered with two fingers without turning. The blue patch in the hood shifted above Jake's head. The market became a line of roofs, then a spread of stalls behind them, then a patch of smoke in the distance.

No one from the shelter stood in the street to witness him go.

It disturbed him for less time than he expected. The road began demanding attention. One wheel struck a stone, the bed jerked, and the wrapped iron gave a muffled answer from its lashings. He set a hand against the nearest crate. The woman with the box walked on the firmer edge of the road, sometimes drawing level with the driver's seat, sometimes falling back when the horses quickened.

Beyond the last market garden, fields opened on either side. Some were planted, others scarred with the stubble of an older harvest. Ditches carried water toward a low stream. A man was repairing a boundary fence with a hammer so small it looked like a child's toy from the road. He did not look up when the cart passed. Nothing on the fence indicated whose land it was. Jake had spent enough time amid the disputed orchard route to be grateful the driver did not offer an opinion.

The cart followed a road where the high ground held a pale, hard surface between the ruts. They stopped once for a team coming down with empty stone baskets. The drivers exchanged news about the condition of the passage farther on. Neither volunteered facts about the shelter, the missing account book, or the stoneworks offer. The world was immense enough to keep whole arguments out of the earshot of men who were moving its goods.

At midday they reached a stand of thin trees above a shallow cut. The driver let the horses drink and checked the chock the daughter had made. It remained in place. He tested the lashings himself, tugging first at the heavy end, then the light. Jake got down and stretched his legs.

The woman with the box sat beneath a tree but did not open it. A man in another wagon offered her water. She accepted. He asked where she was headed. She gave the name of a riverside village Jake had never heard spoken before. When the man asked what she carried, she replied that the contents had already paid their freight. He nodded as though she had answered a different question, which in a way she had.

Jake ate the crust from the cook and kept the rest of his food tied up. The horses lowered their heads. The driver used a blunt knife to clean mud from a strap buckle. He was not a violent or heroic man that Jake could see; he had the alertness of someone responsible for many things that would not explain their failures if asked.

"How far until the works?" Jake asked him.

"Farther than the fare you've paid, if you insist on talking all the way."

Jake laughed. The driver did not, though he looked less annoyed than the words suggested.

When they resumed, the woman remained with her box. The distance between her and the cart kept changing. Nobody offered to turn her into a mystery. By late afternoon the path split, one track descending toward a landing and the other continuing along the higher ground. She waved once to the driver and took the descending track. The cart traveled on. Jake would never know what was inside the box, and the not-knowing did not make the journey incomplete.

### IV. The price of a narrow road

The trouble began where the track narrowed between a bank and a ditch.

There was no collapse, no fallen tree, no enemy waiting in the bend. The outer wheel entered a rut deep enough to tilt the cart bed. The driver halted before the load could shift. The horses stood with their collars taut, feeling the held weight, while a thin stream of mud moved across the tire.

The laborer with the card climbed down and looked under the wheel. He could see the ground beginning to give along the ditch edge. The chock still held the iron, but the whole bed had tilted. The driver told him to fetch a flat stone. There were plenty in the bank. They were damp, irregular, and too small to offer a reliable footing.

A pair of woodcutters approached along the road carrying bundles of short poles. They stopped at a safe distance and watched, not wanting to be drafted into labor that could damage their tools. The driver asked the price of two poles. The older woodcutter named one; the driver tried to reduce it; the young man on the freight list said he would rather pay than lose the axle.

Jake could see the cart's weight waiting above the ditch. The narrow road gave him a memory of the stoneworks kiln passage, where a mistaken command had once made a crew halt an iron frame. The temptation was to issue the signal he remembered and become useful to strangers by borrowing a procedure from another place. But the load, ground and animals here were different. He could tell which problem was urgent without pretending to own the solution.

"What will you put beneath the wheel?" he asked.

The driver pointed with his knife. "A pole to spread the pressure, and stones under the pole. Then we back a hand's width. If the horses bolt, nobody stands in front of them."

The young laborer proposed loosening the iron first to reduce weight. The driver shook his head. Untying it on a slope would turn the cart into a sliding hazard. The woodcutters began to argue about whether their poles would split. One had a knot at the center. They rejected it. The second and third were sound enough for a temporary bed if the driver accepted the risk.

Jake and the laborer carried the poles from the road edge. The woodcutters took payment before letting go of them. Jake found that their caution annoyed him for no good reason. They had cut the wood, carried it, and offered it at a time when it was worth more than it had been an hour earlier. The driver paid because he wanted his horses and goods whole.

Under the driver's directions they laid the poles crosswise against the rut, wedged stones behind them and stepped away. The horses were urged back so slowly that Jake could hear the breath through their nostrils. The wheel moved a finger's width. Mud rose at the edge of the tire. It seemed for a long time that the cart would not move at all.

Then the weight settled onto the makeshift bed with a sound like a wet plank striking another. The driver swore, not out of anger but because he had been holding his breath. They went no farther until he inspected the axle, then tightened a loosened strap, and finally drove across the narrow place while the laborer watched from the bank.

Jake walked beside the team. There was nothing noble in the walk. The mud pulled at his boots, one pole cracked after the rear wheel cleared it, and his left wrist ached from holding stone in place. He would have a bruise there by morning. The woodcutters collected the unbroken pole and moved on, carrying money they had not expected to earn. None of the people involved appeared to regard the incident as a revelation.

At a wider place the driver halted again. The remaining light was thin and the horses were tired. The driver wanted to stop for the night; the laborer with the freight card insisted that one consignment had been promised at the upriver landing before dark. They argued quietly. Jake could not see the account on the card, and neither man showed it to him.

The driver finally announced that they would reach the outer stoneworks yard but not try the passage beyond. That meant the final load would wait until morning, when the track could be examined in proper light. The young laborer spoke of an expected deduction from the freight fee.

"An expected one," the driver said. "It hasn't been taken yet."

Their argument ended there. Jake was glad of the distinction but too tired to repeat it aloud. He had learned that a thing could be delayed without becoming a catastrophe, and that the cost of delay was still real. His fare was spent. No one had offered him road wages. The stoneworks might have work the next morning; the loaded cart would arrive at least an hour later than planned.

They reached the outer yard in dusk. The driver passed beneath a low timber frame with a bell suspended from it. The bell was not struck. Lamps had been set along a platform near the kiln passage, and above the stone buildings the smoke moved sideways instead of rising. The sight troubled Jake more than the rut. He remembered how the men at the works watched smoke as carefully as they watched the iron being carried.

He slid down from the backboard with stiff legs. The road behind them was already hidden by the turn of the bank.

### V. A name beneath a different roof

Deren was waiting under the lamps, not for Jake but for the cart's iron.

He had a cloth across one shoulder and black dust at the edge of his cuffs. Two workers stood near him with a pair of long hooks. They were inspecting the air above the kiln passage. Every few moments a roll of pale smoke appeared beneath the lower eaves and dissolved before it reached the open yard.

Deren greeted the driver, asked whether the load had shifted, and was shown the new wooden chock and the wet mud on the wheels. The driver described the rut with exact indignation. Deren examined the iron and said the cart had been right to stop.

"Can you take it tonight?" the driver asked.

"Not through that passage."

"Then where does it go?"

"On the outer rack. We can account for the pieces in daylight."

The driver looked toward his tired horses. The argument over the freight fee began again in fewer words, with no less force. Deren did not promise payment for delivery into a workshop that had not received the freight. The driver did not promise to bring the cart back without an agreed fee. They arranged to leave the load on its cart until morning, with one man watching the yard. It was not a settlement. It was a way to let everyone sleep without pretending the business had been finished.

Jake stood on the edge of the light with his bundle. He had imagined arriving as a worker, crossing the threshold of a new life with the same certainty he had used to cross the market. Nobody had a reason to stop their tasks to welcome him. It was both disappointing and strangely fair.

When Deren finally saw him he nodded.

"You took the season."

"I took the cart."

"Good. There are two different things to count. We can count the first."

The sentence made Jake smile before he understood that Deren was making no joke. He asked where to put his bundle.

"Sleeping room beyond the dry store. They'll show you the shelf. Bring your own blanket when the evenings get colder. We have more stone than cloth."

Jake followed a worker to a low room with a sloping roof. Eight pallets stood on raised boards, each marked by a different arrangement of boots, cups, folded clothing or tools. The worker indicated an empty space near the far wall and moved a bucket from it.

"That's yours while you're assigned," he said. "Not a deed. Don't leave your knife where someone will stand on it."

Jake set down the bundle. At the shelter he had returned to find his former bed occupied. Here he was given a place without ceremony because another man's absence had made it free. He could not tell who had slept there before. He did not ask.

Outside, the late workers were tending the passage vents. A woman with her hair bound beneath a dark cloth tested the smoke with a strip of pale material held at arm's length. It drew inward and trembled. She told Deren that the upper draw was failing and ordered the nearest man to stop feeding the fire. The man obeyed at once. Nobody offered Jake a heroic task; nobody looked toward him when the air thickened.

The woman examined the passage's small inspection opening, then shut it with a piece of iron. She made a mark beside the door with chalk. The day's work was over for those assigned to the kiln crossing, even though some of them had come expecting paid hours. A young worker swore softly and put away his gloves.

Deren brought Jake to the slate beside the dry store. Under his name was a single line: present. No hours had been entered.

"We'll see what can be done tomorrow," Deren said. "If the draft won't clear, the passage stays shut. You might haul stone elsewhere. You might wait. I told you when you came the first time I wouldn't pay for hours we don't use."

"I remember."

"You should. I don't enjoy saying it twice."

Jake thought of the money he had spent on the fare, the woodcutters' unexpected gain, the daughter collecting for a precisely fitted chock, Esi paying to decide whether the stolen book's cover might be held another day. Everywhere the day had gone, someone had counted something. He had crossed the entire distance and acquired no wage.

He asked whether he could help carry the bags from the cart into the dry store.

"Tomorrow, if the driver and I settle their custody. Leave them tonight."

It was an answer Jake would once have mistaken for a refusal of himself. Now he could feel the fatigue of the road settling into his joints, and it seemed enough to know where the line was drawn. The men with the hooks continued their inspection. Deren moved on to the next argument. Jake had become one working name among many, and for the first evening he was a name with no recorded work.

He ate with the crew at a table scarred by knives and stone grit. Someone told a story about a man who had carried a sack of flour all the way to the wrong gate; the teller spoiled his own ending by laughing too early. Two other men argued about whether the outer roof would leak in the next rain. No one asked Jake to explain the shelter, the missing ledger, or the girl with the saw at the market. He listened and laughed once when the story deserved it.

Later he took the whetstone from its paper and set it on a ledge above the pallet. Len's wrapping had a faint smudge where the old man's injured hand must have pressed too hard while folding it. Jake did not know whether Len had completed the bird after he left. Mara was still with Senn, away on work of her own. Sola had taken her earnings and her tools. The unknown clog remained at the shelter. The ledger's missing leaves and the thief's identity were still unknown; the suspected cover might already have left the west store. The private claimant file and the separate sealed message had not been opened for the sake of this journey. Esi had the keys, the porter had the water path, and the singer's refunds, the old supplier invoice, the unsigned daughter's commission and Jake's partial pledge remained matters of actual work and money, not memories cleansed by distance.

The kiln bells had not sounded. He could hear footsteps in the yard, the scrape of a shovel, and the long, low breathing of tired horses beyond the outer rack. He missed the muffled quarrels of children behind the reed screen. He was surprised by the precision of the missing: not an abstract home, but the spoon with the bent handle, the particular clatter of the serving door, the way the younger porter shifted his carrying pole before the step.

He had left. That was a fact smaller than the future he had imagined, but it could not be argued back into an intention.

From his pallet he watched a little smoke press beneath the eave, then drift away into the night. In the morning it might rise cleanly and let the men work. Or it might not. The stoneworks had not been built to prove that Jake's decision was wise. It would open when the people who knew its passage judged the air fit to breathe.

He put his boots beside the pallet with their soles toward the door. They were still muddy from the narrow road. He left them there.

---

### Editorial handoff — outside the fiction

- **Causal turn:** Jake actually departs the shelter as planned, pays a fare from already earned money, survives a freight-cart delay without acquiring authority over its driver or owner, and reaches Deren's upriver stoneworks. His name is entered as present but no new paid work hours or wages are awarded; the kiln passage is temporarily closed for an observed fictional draft problem. This is a consequential departure and arrival, not another decision deferred to a future chapter.
- **Independent secondary lives:** Esi and the young porter handle the expiring west-store hold without Jake; Sola remains independently employed or absent as she chooses and is not inserted into a rescue plot; the wheelwright daughter negotiates and delivers a fitted paid chock on her own measurements, without certifying her father's unfinished wheel or accepting an unsigned commission; a woman consigning a box travels separately and is not converted into a plot clue. No secondary person receives interior POV.
- **Open loops preserved:** The removed ledger leaves, book thief, west-store cover custody outcome, unidentified clog and stranger, unsafe eastern boundary and the other woman's unretrieved chest, missing claimant's private original and unopened separate message, disputed older invoice, singer refunds, Jake's remaining pledge, unsigned daughter's commission, Mara's absence with Senn, Len's impaired eye and unfinished carving, mill repairs and Sola's future work are NOT solved.
- **Historical provenance:** The old G01 2016 Flaim/ESPN transcription is evidence for competition only and does not authenticate 2016 owner Jake. No new historical score, opponent, trade, champion, route authority, weather fact, legal arrangement or real owner's private motives are asserted. Proposed working roads, kilns, livestock, woodcraft, fares, money, and character history are NONCANON fiction under Atlas/Character review.
- **Review objections:** Check whether an axle-rut recovery duplicates earlier engineered obstacles; whether the daughter's sudden appearance at the weigh-stones is overly convenient; whether the work stoppage delivers adequate dramatic complication without making Jake a passive observer; whether the prose continues to explain its own ethic too overtly. Independent Authors Council, Character Canon, Atlas and Umpire acceptance remain absent. No founder-only promotion, merge or publication is authorized.

# The Board at the Open Door

*2016 Commonwealth historical causal novel — Chapter XXVI candidate. NONCANON / INDEPENDENT EDITORIAL HOLD. Sole limited interior viewpoint: proposed fictional Jake / CHAR-JAKE-KALOPER / PRINCIPAL_CHARACTER, one of the exclusive twelve owner-principal identities. This is NOT an authenticated historical ESPN owner-slot association. All stoneworks, inhabitants, economic customs, signs, trades, roads, wages and dialogue are proposed secondary-world fiction, not approved Character Canon or Atlas facts. No competition result, owner identity, private real-person motive, or new historical claim is asserted. Story-time resumes the morning after Chapter XXV; the protected 2026 Prologue and Chapters I–III are not changed.*

### I. An hour that belonged to somebody

By morning the smoke had stopped coming out beneath the kiln eaves.

That did not mean the passage was open. The air above the yard had the bright, almost empty look that came after a fire was banked. A thin trace of ash settled on the water in the washing basin. Someone had set the work hooks in a row with their points toward the wall, and the racks where the long frames rested stood in a silence that made the previous day's complaints seem far away.

Jake woke to the sound of a cup being put down against stone. The man on the next pallet was sitting with both feet in a bucket. His toes were bare and pale. He held the cup between his knees as though drinking it would require more strength than he had yet acquired.

"You're the one who came on the iron cart," the man said.

"Yes."

"The cart's gone."

Jake sat up. "With the iron?"

"Not the iron. The cart. They took the horses before the feed began charging them another day."

"Is that good?"

"For the horses."

The man resumed examining his feet. He had no intention of supplying the name of the driver, the state of the freight arrangement, or a prediction of Jake's employment. Jake washed, dressed and carried his cup outside. The sky was colorless above the cut of the hill. He heard the distant rise and fall of a saw on the upper shelf, but no hammer pattern from the covered crossing.

At the dry store he found Aren with a board beneath one arm and a piece of bread in the other. The chalk behind Aren's ear was broken at the end. He greeted Jake by looking first at his boots, then at his hands.

"Can you stand a full morning?"

"I came to work."

"That isn't the question."

"Yes," Jake said. "If there's a morning's work."

Aren took the bread from his mouth. "There is work outside the kiln. The rejected roof tiles have to be separated from the ones that can be sold for edging. Noll will show you. Four hours, if you work all four. A meal isn't wages. A place to sleep isn't wages. The kiln is still shut."

"Does the other work pay the same?"

"No. Different order. Different rate. I can tell you the rate or I can tell you to go back to bed."

"Tell me."

Aren did. Jake repeated it so they both knew what had been offered. There was no promise of a following day, and nothing in the offer said he would become a kiln worker when the passage opened. He took the job anyway.

From beyond the low wall came a short disagreement between Vessa and two men carrying a sealed bucket. Vessa was wearing the dark head cloth Jake remembered from his earlier visit. She had a pale strip of material stretched between two wooden pins, and the air moving through it made one corner lift.

"Not through there," she said, pointing toward the passage.

The men turned for the open yard. Vessa followed them to the bend, tested the movement of air again, and made another chalk mark at shoulder height. Jake waited for her to look toward him. She did not. What she knew about the shut passage was her work; what he wished to prove about returning was not evidence she needed.

Noll met him by the outer tile shed, a carrier with a deep crease beside his left eye and a way of speaking before his listener had quite arrived.

"Four hours?"

"That's what Aren said."

"Then let's give him four. We'll save the speeches for when he starts paying by the word."

Inside, racks of tiles stood beneath a roof patched with overlapping strips of wood. Some were pale and square; others carried a narrow lip intended to overlap their neighbors. Several had cracked through the center while drying. Others bore chips that would not be visible when the tile was laid against a garden border. They had been sorted once, perhaps more than once, into stacks that now looked to Jake like the various kinds of somebody else's unfinished argument.

Noll showed him where the usable pieces went, where the broken ones could be crushed for fill, and which batch had been held aside because no buyer had agreed to take it. The pieces did not need to be made heroic. They needed to be lifted without dropping them, inspected in good light, and set where another worker could count them.

Jake took the first from the near stack. It was warmer than the air, though the kiln had not fired it that morning. His thumb found the shallow imprint left by a maker's tool. He turned the piece over and saw a crack reaching to the edge.

"Broken," he said.

Noll held it up to the light. "Yes. You can see that one. It isn't a test."

They worked without much talk. Dust settled on Jake's cuffs. He learned where a sharp lip could abrade the skin between finger and thumb. Noll shifted the stack nearest the door so that the poor light could not disguise an imperfection. Now and then a buyer's cart passed along the high track, its wooden frame knocking against the stones. Jake tried not to follow each sound toward the road.

After a while Noll said, "You keep looking at the entrance."

"I thought Deren might come back."

"If he does, the river will tell us. He's not bringing your first wage in a boat."

Jake smiled despite himself. He had imagined labor beginning with a command loud enough to be heard across the settlement. Instead he was doing something small with someone who had done it before, moving pieces from one side of a shed to the other. Noll was neither impressed nor disappointed. When Jake made an unnecessary second stack, Noll asked him to put it back. When the first hour passed, Noll stopped him long enough to drink.

The useful work was real. For once that fact was not an idea Jake had to defend.

### II. The mark Eren would not erase

Eren came through the outer shed carrying a board nearly as tall as herself.

She had drawn a sun in one corner with dark pigment and filled the lower edge with angular shapes that might have been roofs or steps or the backs of animals. The middle of the board remained bare. In her free hand she carried a cloth bundle tied tight with string. Jake recognized her from the kiln passage, where she had held a lamp by the turn and raised her hand until he obeyed it. Without the lamp she looked like someone headed toward a place that required no signals.

"Vessa needs the last red practice board," Eren told Noll.

"She can have it if she comes and carries the broken half. You know how many boards she's got from me this season?"

"I don't. I came for the one."

Noll indicated a shelf above the rejected tiles. Eren climbed the low step, found a narrow plank stained at one end, and pulled it out. As she did, her larger painted board leaned against a stack. Jake caught it before it could fall.

"That side is wet," she said.

His hand came away black. "Sorry."

"Put it where there's nothing to smear."

He moved it to the doorway. The design faced the daylight. Beneath the sun he could now make out a doorway, a table, and several long rows of figures he had mistaken for roofs. They were not figures exactly. They were strokes of different heights, as though a line of people had left marks on the wood but had not stayed to be counted.

"Is this for the passage?" he asked.

"No."

"Then what is it?"

"A board."

He waited. She returned from the shelf with the red plank beneath her arm and stood beside him. The painted object seemed to make her less ready to speak than the work signal had. In the passage, with a heavy frame waiting, she had known precisely when to lift her hand. Here there were no men she needed to hold.

"It's for the table house on the lower path," she said. "A woman there paid me to paint a sign. People pass the door and think it's a storeroom. It isn't. Anyone can buy a bowl when the table is open. The sign is supposed to say so."

"Do you write the words?"

"Sometimes. She asked for a picture people can read from the path."

He looked again at the shapes. The taller marks were bowls. The doorway was larger than he had first thought. What he had taken for roofs were the folds of a hanging cloth above an open serving table.

"Why so much empty board?"

"Because there has to be a place for the hours." She tapped the bare center with a knuckle. "They change the meal hours. She wants to chalk them in herself and wipe them off. My picture isn't going to promise something the kitchen can't make."

The explanation was ordinary, even shrewd. Jake thought of notices arriving at the shelter with assurances nobody had authorized, and felt his shoulders tighten. He did not tell Eren about them. He was looking at her work, not at a version of his own troubles.

"When does she want it?"

"Today. Before the late meal."

"Will you get there?"

"If people stop asking where I'm going."

She bent to retie the cloth bundle. Inside he saw the ends of two brushes and a narrow jar stopped with a piece of wood. Eren placed the red practice board on Noll's shelf and began to leave, but paused at the opening.

"You're staying here, then."

"For the season."

"That's longer than a morning."

"Usually."

She looked at the tiles, at his hands covered in dust, then at the empty space beyond the shed.

"Aren said he was getting someone for the outside rack. They asked me about the night watch again." She said it without accusation. "I told them I hadn't promised the next season."

Jake remembered Aren saying much the same thing on his earlier visit. Eren would not always be the girl holding the lamp. He had assumed she was being trained for something she had chosen.

"What will you do?"

She looked past him to the hill. "Finish this board first."

Noll came out with another tile and held it where the light crossed the crack. Eren reached to help, then drew back.

"I've got my own work," she said.

"Yes, you have," Noll replied, and set the tile into the broken stack himself.

She went down the path with the large sign carried edge-on to protect its wet paint. The red practice board remained behind. Jake watched the dark corner of the sign disappear past the low wall.

Noll resumed stacking. "Don't make your face like that."

"What face?"

"The one that says you have discovered young people eventually walk away from their elders."

"I wasn't—"

"Good. Then we don't need to talk about it."

But they had not quite returned to work when Vessa entered.

She went straight to the red board. She ran one finger over a mark on the edge and turned it over twice. She looked toward the path Eren had taken.

"Did she bring her other board through here?"

"Yes," Noll said.

"Did she leave it?"

"No."

Vessa stood holding the practice plank. The broken piece was at its upper corner, small enough that Jake might not have seen it if he had not watched her hands. The older woman pressed her thumbnail against the splinter.

"She could have asked me for another piece," she said.

Noll shrugged. "She got what you wanted."

"That isn't what I meant."

She left without explaining what she had meant, and the shed seemed quieter after she was gone. Jake turned a tile over, saw no fault, and put it into the usable stack. He was glad Noll did not ask him to speak for either woman.

### III. What the kiln had never purchased

The four hours ended before the work ended.

Noll stopped when Aren's signal came from the yard. He checked the last stack, refused to estimate the number of pieces they had not finished, and took the tally board himself. Jake washed the clay dust from his hands in cold water. The skin across his palms had grown tender. He had earned the hours; he had not changed what would happen if the kiln remained shut tomorrow.

At the place where the yard opened toward the lower path, Eren was standing with Vessa.

The painted board lay across two wooden stools. Its dark sun was dry now, though the lower border still carried a damp sheen. Vessa held a paintbrush between finger and thumb, as though it were a tool she suspected of belonging to a different trade.

"You took the binding cord," Vessa said.

"I took my cord."

"It was on the passage shelf."

"Because I left it there when we tested the lamp."

"The cord belongs on the shelf until the tests are finished."

"The tests were finished. You watched them finish."

Jake stopped a few steps away. He could have continued to the eating room without becoming part of their quarrel. Eren saw him, however, and raised her chin toward the sign.

"Does the picture look like a table to you?"

"It does now."

"Now?"

"At first I thought it was a row of roofs."

Vessa looked at the painted doorway. Her mouth tightened, though Jake could not tell whether at his honesty or the drawing.

"People need to see a table," Eren said.

"Then make the table larger," Vessa answered.

Eren wiped the brush against her thumb. "If I make it much larger, the woman won't have room for her chalk. I measured the space."

"Then ask the woman to choose what matters."

"She did choose."

Vessa's eyes rose from the board to Eren. "And you have chosen for everyone who needs the lamp in the passage."

That was a different argument. The red practice board had followed her from the shed. It rested against the stool, splintered corner up. Jake understood now that the quarrel might not have been about cord or paint at all.

"I trained another caller," Eren said. "I left the patterns. I am not the only person who can read a hand."

"Not the only one," Vessa agreed. "The one who knows the recess when the lamp is out, though."

"Then they should train that person."

Vessa placed the brush on the edge of the board. "Do you want to stop the watch?"

"I want to do this work."

"You have always done work."

"Work you gave me."

Eren spoke quickly, then drew back a little, as though the words had come out before she could decide which ones she meant. Jake felt the impulse to move between them. It was the familiar feeling that a conversation might be repaired if he found the right sentence before either person became angrier. He had once interrupted a harder conversation at the shelter for just that reason and made it worse.

Vessa did not ask for his assistance. She looked at Eren for a long moment.

"Was the commission paid before you began?"

"Part of it."

"Will the rest be paid before you hang it?"

"Yes."

"Do you know that, or has the buyer told you it?"

"The buyer told me. I made her repeat it in front of her sister."

Vessa's shoulders shifted, almost imperceptibly. She lifted the practice board from the ground and held it against her side.

"I taught you to ask that question when somebody wants a thing carried through the passage."

"You taught me to stop when I couldn't see the next person," Eren replied. "I learned the rest elsewhere."

Vessa could have taken offense. Jake could see nothing in her face that settled the matter. The mark of her thumb remained on the red board. A fly landed on the wet corner of the painted sign and walked away with a little pigment on its legs.

After a silence Vessa said, "The watch is covered tonight. It might not be next month. If you're leaving the training, I need to know what I can count on."

"I am taking the sign today," Eren said. "I told Aren yesterday. I didn't promise the next season. I can call the passage when I'm assigned until then."

"That wasn't an answer to everything."

"It was an answer to what I can promise."

Vessa looked down at the sign. "The table ought to be a little larger."

Eren gave a laugh, short and startled.

"It's already dry."

"Then it will stay the size it is."

Vessa returned the brush. She picked up the red board and walked toward the passage. Nothing had been forgiven in a large, visible way. The watch remained covered tonight; the next season remained unsettled. Eren still had a commission due before the late meal.

Jake found himself suddenly tired. He was not the center of this encounter, and it had cost him something to understand that. He had heard Vessa say what she could not count on. He had heard Eren name the precise limit of what she was giving. Neither had been persuaded by a speech. The important thing between them was still moving.

Eren lifted the sign.

"You said it wasn't a table at first," she told him.

"I did."

"Come down the path and look at it from where the buyers will stand."

"Are you asking me to carry it?"

"No. I can carry it. I want to know if you see a table before you see a roof."

He looked toward the meal room and the cup he had left there. His four hours were finished. The walk was his own to choose. Eren did not wait to be rescued from the weight of her board.

"All right," he said.

### IV. The house with a door but no name

They went down by the steps where Jake had once watched a girl carry a pot, past a wall washed white in patches and a stand of low shrubs. The path narrowed beside a row of houses whose thresholds stood above it on uneven stones. Eren kept the painted board close to her body when children passed, turning it so the wet edge faced away from their hands. At the bend she stopped and made Jake walk ahead.

"Look back," she said.

He turned. From three paces away the image was plain enough: a table beneath an open cloth, a bowl on either side, a square of untouched wood above. The dark sun marked the direction of the high light rather than the sun's actual place. He could see now how she had made the cloth fold outward to suggest that the door stood open.

"I see it," he said.

"Even the bowls?"

"One looks a little like a stone."

"That's because you spent all morning looking at stones."

She looked pleased at her own answer, then frowned at the left-hand bowl. She set the sign against a wall and took a brush from the bundle. With two small strokes she deepened the line beneath the rim. No master craftsman came down to bless the correction. She did not ask Jake whether it was better.

The table house stood where the lower path widened enough for two carts to pass without one climbing the bank. It had no sign above the door, only a worn place where something had once been fixed. Inside, several people were washing bowls at a long trough. The smell of onions and hot fat came through the entrance. A woman with strong forearms stood at the threshold and called to Eren before they reached it.

"You've brought the board."

"I said I would."

"Did you finish the empty middle?"

"I left it empty for the hours."

The woman rubbed her hands against a cloth. "My sister said you wouldn't remember."

"Your sister was there when we agreed."

"I know. I'm telling you what she said."

Eren set the board upright so the woman could examine it. Jake stood off to the side, suddenly aware that he had followed Eren from the hill without being invited into the household business. A child inside the kitchen made a row of spoons ring against the table. Somebody told the child to stop, not unkindly. The buyer ran her finger along the lower painted edge but did not touch the wet bowl.

"Where will you put the day's hours?" she asked.

Eren handed her a small piece of chalk. "Here. You can make them large enough to read from the turn. If you change them, wipe this space, not the picture. The pigment has to dry before anybody washes the whole thing."

The woman tested the chalk in the bare center. She wrote a short line of marks, looked from the path, then rubbed the marks away with the hem of her cloth. The panel took the writing and released it. That, more than the painting, appeared to satisfy her.

"I promised the rest when you brought it," the woman said.

Eren waited while the woman went inside. A younger woman emerged carrying a narrow bag tied at the neck. The first buyer opened it and counted out the payment into Eren's palm. Jake could not tell the amount. Eren counted it again, quietly, before putting it in her own pouch.

"Do you want it hung now?" Eren asked.

"The pegs are already there."

Eren looked up. The old fixing holes had been fitted with wooden pegs, and two short loops hung above them. She drew the sign upward, tested the loops with her fingers, and slid the board into place. It sat a little lower on one side. She took it down, shortened one loop, and hung it again. This time the painted table faced the path without leaning.

The work was finished.

It was not a public triumph. A man passing with a bundle of laundry stopped long enough to read the unwritten center, saw nothing there, and went on. The younger woman took a bowl back inside. The buyer stood beside Eren and looked at her new sign, then immediately began speaking about what she would put in tomorrow's soup.

Eren said the final pigment needed another day before hard rain. The woman said they could take it under the eave if a storm came. They discussed where to keep the chalk. No one asked Jake to choose. He was glad of it.

When the women went inside to begin their work, Eren remained on the path. She turned once toward the sign, then looked at Jake.

"Well?"

"It says what you wanted."

"What did I want?"

"For people to know where to eat."

She shook her head. "For her to be able to tell them when. A painted feast wouldn't have helped if she had nothing to serve."

He looked again at the empty center. A person could fill it each day and erase it each night. The drawing would remain, but the promise would be new each time, made by the person who could keep it. He recognized the discipline of that without needing Eren to explain any further.

"Will Vessa like it?" he asked.

Eren put away her brushes. "She doesn't have to."

They walked back uphill at different speeds. Eren had another stop to make near the upper shelf; she told him so when they reached the turning. He neither offered to go with her nor asked whom she intended to see. She raised a hand, the same hand he had once obeyed in the kiln passage. This time it was farewell, not an order to hold.

Jake climbed the remaining steps alone. Below him the board hung above the open door. He could not read the empty center from where he stood. From the path, where the people who might actually buy a meal would pass, it was large enough.

### V. A day's wages

Aren was still at the dry store when Jake returned.

He had changed his coat. The chalk behind his ear had broken again, and he was holding the day board under the light from the open doorway. Noll had already brought the tile tally. There was a column for the damaged pieces and another for those whose edges could be sold. Jake was relieved to see his work recorded in a place that did not expect him to make it sound more important than it had been.

"You stayed the full four," Aren said.

"Yes."

"Noll says you know how to sort the lip from the crack."

"I knew by the end."

"Noll thinks the first two stacks were slower than they needed to be."

"They were."

Aren counted out the agreed payment for four hours of outside work. The sum was less than Jake could have earned on the closed kiln passage, had it been safe and had he been assigned there. It was more than he had possessed that morning. He held the coins briefly, feeling their different edges through the dust that remained on his fingers.

The shelter came to mind: Esi with the replacement leaves, the young porter lifting the water pole, the incomplete reed screen, the debt that had survived every good intention. His first urge was to divide the money immediately into the amount he owed and the amount that would prove he remembered. Then he remembered that he had no way to send the money that night, and that the people at the shelter needed an actual delivery more than an imagined payment.

At the evening meal Noll ate across from him. The man from the neighboring pallet sat at the end of the bench, his feet now wrapped in clean cloth. Somebody passed a bowl of beans along the table; a younger worker took two helpings and was told to leave some for the people still outside. There was no argument beyond that. A spoon struck the rim of a cup. No one stopped to listen for an instruction.

"First four?" Noll asked.

"First four here."

"Then don't call them a season."

"I wasn't going to."

"Good. We get enough talk from men who have bought a blanket and think they've purchased the hill."

Jake looked toward the door. He had not bought a blanket yet. The one he carried from the shelter was thin, and the nights in the sleeping room were colder than he had expected. He had intended to save every coin he could. It annoyed him that a practical need should rise so quickly to challenge a promise made far away.

After eating he went to the small market shelf beyond the upper yard. An older vendor had spread folded cloth and worn traveling wraps beneath a canvas awning. Jake chose a plain covering that had been patched twice. He tested the seam and asked its price. The vendor held to her first number until he pointed out a third repair near the hem; then she reduced it a little. He paid. The purchase took some of the day's wage, though not all.

He carried the folded cloth back under his arm. It was not a gift for the shelter, not proof of a settled account, not a reward for virtue. It was something he needed to sleep beneath while earning the money he had said he would earn. He disliked how selfish the purchase felt even after he had made it. He disliked even more the thought of making himself ill to win an argument with a memory of Esi.

At the passage entrance he found Vessa sitting on a low block with the red practice board across her knees. She was repairing its splintered corner with a strip of cloth and a small amount of glue. The kiln door remained marked shut. The faint smell of the cooling fires had not changed.

"Did Eren hang her sign?" Vessa asked.

"She did."

"Did the woman pay her?"

"Yes."

Vessa pressed the cloth against the board. "Then it was work."

Jake sat on the opposite block, careful to keep his new covering clear of the glue.

"She said she hadn't promised the watch next season."

"She hasn't."

"Will you find someone else?"

"I'll train whoever agrees to train."

Vessa said no more. A few steps away the path curved toward the dark crossing. Its silence held a labor Jake had been eager to perform and had not been permitted to perform. Vessa had spent a day protecting people from it without earning his admiration in any official way. She had asked about Eren's payment before she asked about the painting. He could not tell whether she approved, only that she had listened.

"Is the air any better?" he asked.

"It is different. Better isn't a measure. We test it again before anybody carries iron."

He nodded.

"Are you here tomorrow?" she asked.

"If Aren has the hours."

"He might have something outside. The crossing will stay shut until I say otherwise."

"I understand."

"No," Vessa said, quite gently. "You know that I said it. Understanding will take more than one day."

She kept working on the red plank. Jake looked at the board where Eren's fingers had once marked a hold. It would go back into service even if she did not. The board at the table house had gone into service too, carrying a different kind of invitation. He had watched one woman leave a practice board behind and another woman keep it alive. The two acts were neither betrayal nor agreement.

In the sleeping room he spread the new covering over the thin one. It settled around the edges of the pallet with the dusty smell of cloth that had been stored near a road. His boots remained beneath the shelf. The whetstone Len had lent him lay wrapped in paper beside his cup.

He took out a sheet and began a letter to Esi.

He wrote that he had arrived. He wrote that the kiln passage was closed and that he had been paid for four hours in the outer tile shed. He did not write that the wages were on their way, because they were not. He did not say what the shelter ought to do with its stolen book or that Mara would return. The ink caught on the roughness of the page, leaving a dark spot after the last word.

He read the letter once. It sounded like a report from a man who had come away to become exact. He almost tore it up. Instead he added a sentence about the bent spoon at the shelter, which he still missed more than he had expected. The sentence was foolish, perhaps. It was also one the people who had eaten beside him might understand.

No cart was leaving for the lower market that night. He folded the letter and put it under the whetstone, unsealed. The coins not spent on the covering went into the bottom of his bag. They did not pay the pledge by being saved.

Outside, a worker passed his door singing two notes of a tune and then forgetting the rest. The singer laughed at himself and tried again. Jake lay under the warmer cloth. He did not know the tune. For the first time since the cart had carried him beyond the weigh-stones, he could allow a day to be complete without making it the first day of a different man.

He had been paid for four hours. Eren's sign was hanging over a real doorway. The kiln was still closed. Tomorrow was not written anywhere he could read.

---

### Editorial handoff — outside the fiction

- **Causal turn:** Jake performs four actual paid hours of modest tile-sorting labor in the outside shed, receives only the agreed outside-work wage, buys a necessary secondhand covering from part of it, and retains the remainder without pretending to have sent or paid his shelter pledge. The earlier closed kiln passage stays closed under Vessa's independent safety authority. This is not a mechanical rescue or another deferred decision about whether to leave.
- **Independent secondary arc:** Eren independently finishes, hangs and receives the balance of payment for a reversible meal-hours sign at an existing proposed table house; its picture cannot itself promise food or opening hours. She and Vessa openly disagree over next season's watch and the extent of an apprentice's obligations. Eren has NOT pledged the next season, has NOT declared herself gone, and retains her own commissions; Vessa retains the passage safety authority and the red training board. No Eren, Vessa, Aren or Noll interior POV is entered.
- **Open loops held:** The four previously unpaid arrival hours remain zero (Jake only works today); the kiln remains closed; Eren's next-season plans are unresolved; the stoneworks season is not guaranteed beyond hours actually assigned. Esi's shelter accounts, removed ledger leaves and unidentified thief, uncertain west-store cover custody, private claimant file and sealed separate message, unrelated clog/stranger, Sola's remaining screen panel, Mara with Senn, Len's damaged eye/carved bird, unsound boundary and locked chest, disputed supplier invoice, singer's incomplete refunds, wheelwright daughter's unsigned commission, and Jake's remaining pledge are not resolved. The letter to Esi is UNSENT.
- **Historical boundary:** Retained 2016 source records do not authenticate the proposed Jake owner slot. The scene asserts no ESPN score, team ownership, title, trade, matchup, historical weather, geographical fact or real-person psychology. Economic rates, shop work, kiln physics, art signs, fictional persons and settlement relations remain noncanonical and subject to Character Canon and Atlas review.
- **Independent review challenges:** Examine whether the scene overexplains the ethic of bounded work; whether Eren's sign commission is sufficiently dramatic without imposing an invented quasi-governmental institution; whether Vessa's authority and Eren's independence are both legible through observable behavior; whether the kiln's closure and Jake's first paid shift remain temporally coherent; and whether the chapter's quieter pace helps rather than stalls the twenty-five-chapter narrative. No independent Authors Council, Character Canon, Atlas, Umpire or Founder-only approval has occurred; no founder-only promotion, merge or publication is authorized.
