# The Room with No High Seat

*2016 Commonwealth historical causal novel — Chapter XXVII candidate. NONCANON / INDEPENDENT EDITORIAL HOLD. Sole limited interior viewpoint: proposed fictional Jake / CHAR-JAKE-KALOPER / PRINCIPAL_CHARACTER, one of the twelve exclusive owner-principal identities. This is NOT an authenticated historical ESPN owner-slot association. All hill settlement customs, buildings, economies, people, conversations, journeys and labor arrangements are proposed secondary-world fiction; no real historical matchup, owner binding, trade, score, outcome or private motive is asserted. The protected 2026 Prologue and Chapters I–III are untouched. Story-time begins the day after Chapter XXVI. Neither Character Canon, Atlas, independent Authors Council, Umpire nor Founder has approved this draft.*

### I. The bread at the edge of the bench

The covering had kept him warm, and he woke late enough to feel ashamed of it.

Nothing in the sleeping room was late. The men who had gone to the lower quarry had left their folded blankets on the shelves; a kettle waited by the cold hearth; one long stripe of pale light lay across the floor where the threshold stone had worn lower than its neighbors. The man on the next pallet was not there. His foot bucket was empty, and on the rim someone had balanced the cup he had been examining the previous morning.

Jake pulled his boots on and found the letter beneath Len's whetstone. He had not sealed it. The thin paper had taken on the faint curve of the shelf. It said what he had written: that he had arrived, worked four paid hours, bought something to sleep beneath, and remembered the bent spoon at the shelter. It said almost nothing about the people to whom he had promised help. He ran a finger along the fold, unwilling either to add another apology or to make the letter more impressive.

Outside, Noll sat beneath the tile shed's eaves breaking a hard loaf in two. He had not set out tools. The racks stood in their morning rows, the better edging pieces in a short stack under a cloth. No smoke came from the kiln crossing. Vessa's strip of pale material hung beyond it, lifted at one corner by a movement of air Jake could not see.

"Did Aren put me on the shed again?" Jake asked.

Noll offered half the bread. "You're not on anything today. He sent the orders up yesterday. The buyers haven't agreed to take another stack until they see the last one."

"I can move the broken fill."

"You could. Who'd pay you for it?"

"I wasn't asking for much."

"That isn't the same as having an order."

Jake held the bread without eating. He had come for a season, which he had silently translated into a sequence of mornings that would pay him. One small day's wages had barely survived a purchase he needed. The blank day now seemed to press against his ribs as firmly as an unpaid debt.

"You can sit," Noll said. "That's one of the things nobody has forbidden."

Jake sat on a low block. The bread was dry but had a good dark crust. He watched Noll arrange his portion on a cloth before eating, as carefully as if he had been given a plate.

"Where's the man with the bad feet?"

"At his sister's. He goes there on his off days. He has more than the two feet you saw."

"He didn't say he had a sister."

"He doesn't have to give you a family tree to share a pallet."

Noll's voice carried no rebuke, yet Jake felt the small annoyance of being treated like an impatient child. He tore off a piece of bread and found he was hungry.

For several minutes they listened to the ridge. A distant hammer began, then stopped. Below, a cart's wheels rattled across a piece of hard ground. The open hill existed without offering work to him, and at last he could hear that the quiet was not an accusation.

"There's a table in the lower house this evening," Noll said. "They've been talking about it since Eren put up her board."

"A meal?"

"A meal if the cook has made one. A place to sit if she hasn't. Most people bring a little something. Some bring nothing and pretend they were invited for their stories. Don't ask the cook which sort you are."

"I don't know anyone there."

"You know me."

"Well enough to eat your bread."

"That is about as much as most people know." Noll took back the cloth, brushed the crumbs into his hand and ate them. "Come if you like. No one keeps the high seat."

Jake supposed the phrase meant something to the people of the hill. Perhaps someone had once claimed the best chair. Perhaps a rule had grown out of a quarrel that no one living could explain. He could ask. Instead he looked toward the road. He had no work and no right to pretend he had a wage coming, but the invitation made him feel, for an instant, that an empty day might be spent without being wasted.

"What do they talk about?"

"Whatever people bring. Sometimes the talk's better than the food. Sometimes a person thinks he was invited to talk and discovers what people wanted was for him to listen."

Noll rose. He was going to help a cousin mend a roof below the ridge; he would receive dinner there but no wage. Jake had been about to ask how a man could afford a day like that. The question sounded different after Noll said it aloud. A cousin's roof, a table after dark, a loaf divided on a step: not every exchange had been entered in the accounts Jake had begun to carry in his head.

When Noll went, Jake sat another moment. The hill did not owe him a second day's work. That did not mean he was required to fill the hours with proof of regret.

He put the letter back in his bag. At the bottom, the remaining coins knocked softly together. He might spend one on a place at the table. He might keep them for the shelter. There were choices he could not settle by making them sound like accounts.

### II. A sign that was not an invitation

The lower table house stood with its door open to the path, exactly as Eren had painted it. In daylight the board was brighter than Jake remembered. The sun at its corner had dried to a muted ochre, and the drawn doorway held a stripe of black that made the actual darkness behind the threshold seem intentional. Across the bare center someone had chalked the evening's hour. A hand below it had written that the kitchen might close early if the lentils ran out.

The woman at the doorway wore a narrow scarf tied twice behind her neck. Her sleeves were rolled back from her forearms. Jake recognized her from the day Eren delivered the sign, when she had counted the balance of the payment into the painter's palm. Now she held a basket of onions with a ripped handle.

"You came to read my board," she said.

"I came for the table."

"Then you've read enough. Take the other handle."

He caught the basket before the bottom struck his knee. She led him inside, through the room where the long tables stood, and into a narrow yard. There was a chopping block and an earthenware basin, a heap of onion skins and a stout little tree with its leaves stripped by goats on the side closest to the wall. The woman set down her end of the basket.

"I'm Fara," she said. "I paid for the board. That's my part of the story if somebody asks. What's yours?"

"Jake."

"Are you one of Vessa's people?"

"I worked four hours for Aren yesterday. In the outside shed."

"Then you're one of the people whose shift is uncertain."

"Yes."

"Good. We know that much." She pulled an onion from the basket and put it into his hand. "You may help if you like. Or you may go sit. I won't have people claiming I made them work for their supper."

He looked down at the onion. It was smaller than the ones the shelter received on good weeks, and it had a dark place near its root.

"Where should the peels go?"

"In the basket by the wall. That's a question I like hearing."

They stood together cutting the onions. Fara worked quickly with a narrow knife that had been sharpened far down its blade. Jake worked slowly, not because he feared the knife but because his eyes had begun to sting. When the first bowl filled, Fara carried it inside.

He followed her into a room that had not been made to impress a visitor. The walls were pale except where years of hands had darkened the lime near the doors. Two windows opened toward the path, both with a ledge broad enough to hold a cup. The benches were not quite of one height. One had been mended with a plank so new that its raw edge stood out against the older wood. A person who cared to arrange people by rank would have found nothing in the furniture to help him.

Three workers were already seated. One was a woman Jake had seen bringing water to the kiln yard; another had his arm in a sling. Their conversation ran across Jake without slowing. They were discussing a mule that had escaped from a cart and spent half a morning standing outside the bread stall as though waiting to be served. The mule had been caught, but nobody agreed on who had caught it.

"Don't sit there," Fara said, as Jake approached an empty place by the window.

"Is that the high seat?"

"That's where the roof drips when the wind shifts. You can take it if you like, but I don't want to be blamed when your trousers are wet."

The room's laughter was small and unceremonious. Jake chose a place beside the mended bench. Nobody asked what he had earned, what he owed, or how many people depended on him. For a moment this seemed like a mercy. Then he wondered what he would have to say if they did ask.

At the far end a young worker began tapping a spoon against a jug. His name, someone said, was Pell. He wanted to know whether Vessa had tested the crossing again.

"She has," the woman with the water buckets replied.

"And?"

"She hasn't opened it."

"She'll test it until we die of old age. I could have carried iron yesterday."

"You could have gone down the open path and offered to carry it somewhere else," the woman said.

"That isn't the work they owe me."

"They don't owe you a closed passage opened."

The exchange moved lightly at first, like a game familiar to both sides. But Pell did not smile. Jake watched the spoon leave his fingers and settle on the table. He had been paid in the outside shed; Pell had apparently not been offered work there. A dispute about the passage that had looked, from Jake's side, like a question of safety had acquired another weight.

Fara set a pot on the serving board. She had heard every word.

"Not while people are eating," she said.

Pell looked at her. "It's not just your supper."

"No. But it is my room."

He took his hand from the table and folded his arms. Nobody moved to defend him. Nobody moved to answer him, either. Fara filled bowls, leaving the pot lid tilted so the steam could escape. Jake accepted a portion and thanked her. The lentils were thin, with pieces of onion and a bitter leaf he could not name. He ate as though hunger, at least, required no explanation.

### III. What a closed door cost

Vessa came in after the first bowls had been emptied.

She stood a moment by Eren's sign, brushing dust from the edge of her head cloth. Eren followed a few steps behind, carrying the red practice board beneath one arm. She had brought it not because she needed a lesson at supper but because Vessa was carrying a small bundle of tools and there had not been a free hand between them. Jake understood this only when Eren leaned the board against the wall and returned the bundle.

"There's room," Fara said.

"Only if you haven't filled the last bowl with the ladle," Vessa replied.

Fara put a bowl on the table. "The ladle eats last."

There was room enough for two people to sit near the door. Eren took one place, Vessa the other. They did not sit as a pair. The younger woman looked at the painted sign in the open doorway and seemed amused to see it working without her. Vessa looked toward the men who were still standing.

Pell's voice changed. "You heard what we were talking about."

"I heard you before I came through the door," Vessa said.

"Then answer."

"Ask me something I can answer."

Pell rose. He was taller than Jake had thought, with a white patch of dried clay on one knee. "How many shifts do you expect us to lose?"

"I don't know."

"How long before the passage is safe?"

"I don't know."

"Does Aren pay you to say you don't know?"

A spoon rang against a bowl. The young man in the sling moved his chair back a little, making a narrow space between himself and Pell. Jake understood that everyone at the table knew a history between these two that he had not been told.

Vessa kept her hands in her lap. "He pays me to know when I have a measure and when I haven't got one. You can ask him whether he thinks that's worth paying for."

"Easy for you. You're being paid."

"Yes."

"Some of us were counting on that work."

"I know."

"You don't know what I was counting on."

"No," Vessa said. "I don't."

The reply did not soothe Pell. Its exactness seemed to make him angrier. He began naming things: the room he shared with two cousins, the price of dry fuel, the cloth his mother had been promised. None of them was a gesture. Jake could imagine the roof of the shared room, the soot on the fuel, the mother's hand expecting cloth. Pell had counted his future in particular objects. Each day the kiln remained shut, one of those objects moved farther out of reach.

Eren rested her hand on the red board where it leaned against the wall. She did not interrupt. Jake had expected her to speak on Vessa's behalf, perhaps because he had seen her learning the safety signs. Instead she watched Pell with a frank attention that was almost painful to witness.

Pell turned suddenly toward Jake.

"You were there. You saw it. Tell her the smoke had gone."

Every face moved with the turn. Jake's own memory rose to meet them: the still air at the eaves, Vessa's strip of material lifting, the sealed bucket turned away, the closed door. He could say that the smoke was no longer rising in the open air. He could say nothing about what gathered inside the crossing.

"The smoke above the eaves was less," he said.

Pell opened his hands. "There."

"But I didn't measure the air in the passage. Vessa did."

"You're one of her friends now?"

"I worked outside for four hours. That's all I can tell you."

Pell looked as though he had been offered a broken tool. "I didn't ask how many hours you worked."

"I know."

"Then why do you keep saying it?"

Because, Jake thought, if I tell you I know more, I can borrow some of the importance of your anger. He did not speak the thought. Pell had no use for a stranger's lesson in honesty. He needed wages. The closed passage, regardless of its reason, had put distance between him and the things he meant to buy.

"Enough," Fara said.

Pell shook his head. "People get to say enough when they've had their meal."

"I served yours," Fara replied. "It isn't going anywhere. Sit and finish it, or leave it. You don't get to make the rest of us fight your fight for you."

Eren stood. For an instant Jake thought she meant to defend Vessa after all. Instead she picked up the red board and carried it to the wall behind the serving table, away from Pell's feet.

"That's mine to carry," Vessa said quietly.

"I know. I don't want it broken."

Pell sat. The movement looked less like obedience than exhaustion. Around the room the talk recommenced in low fragments, but the evening had shifted. Jake could hear the scrapes of spoons, the settling of the new bench, the little rasp of Fara's knife cutting bread into uneven pieces. He had given a true answer. It had helped nobody feel better. He did not think that made it less necessary, only less satisfying than he had hoped.

### IV. The price of being heard

When the last of the lentils had been divided, Fara set a shallow plate on the table for anyone who had money to spare for the kitchen. She put no person at its side to watch the giving. Jake reached toward his bag, hesitated, and set one small coin on the plate. It was part of the four hours he had earned, and once placed there it could not also go toward the shelter.

He almost took it back.

Noll, who had arrived late with rain spots on one sleeve though no rain had fallen at the table house, saw his hand stop.

"You're allowed to pay for your supper," he said.

"I have other things to pay."

"So do the rest of us."

Noll placed a piece of dry cheese beside the plate, wrapped in cloth. Fara took it to the kitchen without thanking him in a way anyone could hear. The exchange had evidently occurred before, often enough not to need ceremony.

People began leaving in twos and threes. Pell remained at the far end, finishing the cold food he had nearly abandoned. The woman with the water buckets drew up a chair beside him. Jake could not hear what she said, only that Pell did not send her away. Vessa was standing by the doorway, collecting her tools. Eren had carried the red board outside.

Noll motioned toward the yard.

"You could walk with me," he said. "I have to fetch the cloth I left at my cousin's, and your bed's on the same hill."

They stepped into the air. The path held the faint smell of cooked onions. The light had thinned to the point where the stones could no longer be distinguished by color. The eaves of the table house glowed behind them from a single lamp.

"Do they always argue like that?" Jake asked.

"Some nights they're kind to each other. Some nights somebody starts counting what the rest of them owe."

"Pell isn't wrong about losing work."

"No."

"And Vessa isn't wrong to close the passage."

"Perhaps not."

Jake waited for the older man to offer an answer worth remembering. Noll found a loose stone in the path with his toe and pushed it to the side so that someone coming down would not stumble on it.

"My cousin has a roof that leaks near the cook place," he said. "I spent the morning helping mend it. We moved the same strip of board three times. Each time he wanted it in a different place. I was half ready to throw him off his own ladder."

"What did you do?"

"Waited until he told me where the water actually came in. He had been showing me where he thought it came in. The difference took us the whole morning."

"Did you fix it?"

"We put a temporary cover over the seam. When it rains hard, we'll find out whether we were right."

Jake smiled. Noll stopped him with a look.

"That's not a lesson about Vessa. Don't make my cousin's roof into a speech. He needs the roof. Pell needs the money. Vessa needs to be right about the air. They are three different needs that happen to be making a noise together."

At the turn in the path a small procession of people moved past them, carrying baskets toward the table house. One had a child asleep against his shoulder. A woman said that the kitchen was probably closed. Another replied that she knew and was going to leave something for morning. It did not seem to occur to them that a shut door meant the room had ceased to exist.

Noll watched them go. "If you sit with people long enough, you don't have to make every hurt belong to you."

Jake thought of Esi reconstructing the book, of Sola choosing to return only for her own work, of Mara leaving with a sister whose life Jake barely knew. All these people had had business in which he was not the measure. He had understood that in sentences, sometimes even in deeds. But the impulse to arrange their suffering around the story of his improvement remained strong. It was possible to act modestly and still make a performance of modesty.

At his sleeping-room door Noll turned toward the other path.

"Why did you invite me?" Jake asked.

"Because you were sitting on a block looking as though you'd forgotten how to stop earning." Noll shrugged. "And because there's a place when you come."

"Will Pell be there tomorrow?"

"If there's food. I don't know if he'll speak to Vessa."

"Do you?"

"Not about her passage. I know about my cousin's roof."

Noll went away without ceremony. Jake watched until the older man's shape disappeared behind the low wall. His own day had no entry on Aren's wage board. He had spent a coin on food he might have eaten elsewhere for less. He had also been present when a man described the cloth he could not afford for his mother. Jake could not turn that man into a useful emblem. The cloth was still missing.

Inside, the folded letter waited in his bag. He spread it on the shelf and read it once more. For the first time he saw that the line about the bent spoon was the only line that expected Esi to be a person rather than an account keeper. He wished he could add a question without making the answer a burden.

He wrote beneath it: *How are you sleeping?* Then he sat with the pen in his hand. It was a question he could not demand an answer to, and it was not one Esi could settle by filling in a figure on her rebuilt leaves.

The night moved around him. Beyond the sleeping room a cart arrived, tired animals shifting under the straps. Someone called out the name of a missing wheel pin. The world continued with problems he could not inspect, mend or claim.

He folded the letter along the original marks.

### V. A journey without him

In the morning he took the letter to Fara's table house before the ovens were warm.

The board stood in the doorway. Someone had wiped away the evening hour, leaving a faint crescent of chalk where the wet cloth had missed the corner. Eren's painted sun held its color. The building looked shut until Fara opened the door, but it was not abandoned. Jake could hear a bucket being drawn in the yard and the familiar sound of a knife against a block.

"You came early," Fara said. "I have yesterday's bread if you came to be fed."

"I wondered whether anyone takes letters downriver."

"People take anything if you can tell them where it goes. That doesn't mean it arrives."

He told her the shelter's name and the lower road that led toward it, leaving out the stories that made the place matter to him. She listened with her head tilted slightly. Then she called through the yard to a woman who was loading folded baskets onto a handcart. Her name was Mev, and she carried small private packets as part of her trading journey. Fara did not introduce her as a sure way home. Mev was going only as far as the market beyond the ferry, where she sometimes passed messages to a westward carrier. The remaining road was someone else's business.

"You can give it to me," Mev said, examining the envelope without touching its seal. "I can say where I carried it. I can't say whose hands take it after."

"How much?"

She told him. He looked at the coins left in his bag. The charge would reduce his remaining wage to a smaller amount than he had meant to keep. He could afford it if the next day's work came. He could not afford it with the certainty he wanted.

"Would you send it without the fee?" he asked.

Mev looked at him for a long moment. There was no outrage in her face. Only the attention of someone being asked to give up something she had already worked to arrange.

"No."

"I understand."

"Most people do when they ask twice."

She set the letter on the edge of her cart while he counted the payment, but did not tuck it among the packets until the coins were in her hand. Then she placed it beneath a square of dry cloth and tied the bundle at two corners. The letter was no longer on Jake's shelf. It had not reached Esi. Between those facts lay a road of which he knew only the first portion.

"What's your name?" he asked.

"Mev."

"If someone asks who carried it—"

"Tell them I took it as far as I said I would. Don't make me the rest of the road."

She returned to securing the baskets. Fara came out with a wrapped loaf and gave it to her, then took a different packet from the cart. Neither announced what was in it. Jake felt the desire to ask and let it go.

He walked back up the hill without purchasing breakfast. The morning light showed a little frost-colored dust where the quarry track met the yard. At the stoneworks the kiln crossing remained closed. Vessa's strip of material was still in place. Near the outer shed, Aren was speaking to two buyers beside the edging tiles. Jake waited until they finished; he had learned enough not to interrupt a price being settled.

Aren turned. "Nothing for you this morning. Perhaps there'll be carrying after the next cart comes."

"Will there be pay if the cart doesn't come?"

"No."

Jake nodded. He had wanted a better answer, one that would let him count the cost of Mev's journey as an investment rather than a risk. Aren was already reaching for a chalk stub.

From the lower path came a sound he could not at first identify: a handful of people laughing, not loudly, their words lost as they passed behind the high wall. He thought of Pell and the cold lentils, of Eren's painted board, of Vessa taking the full weight of a stopped passage without pretending the stopped wages were harmless. He thought of the question on the page now moving away from him.

He did not know whether Esi was sleeping. He did not know whether Pell had found work, whether the roof Noll had covered would hold, or whether a messenger he had paid would find another messenger willing to carry his words. His ignorance was not a great moral discovery. It was the condition under which those people lived their lives, whether he approved of it or not.

In the shed a worker called for help shifting a light rack from the doorway. Jake went to the threshold and asked whether the carrying was assigned paid work. The worker said it was only to clear the passage for the next delivery, and nobody had authorized hours for it.

Jake moved the rack with him anyway, a short lift into a place that would keep the doorway clear. He did not write it down as a day's labor. He did not expect it to repay the coin spent on supper or the messenger's charge. The other worker thanked him and went back to his own task.

When the rack stood clear, Jake stepped outside. The closed kiln had not opened; the letter had not arrived; the stoneworks had not promised him a season of days. At the lower table there would be people with names he had only begun to learn, and none of them had appointed him to explain what their lives meant.

He took the empty morning as it was, with a little less money and an actual message on its way.

---

### Editorial handoff — outside the fiction

- **Dramatic question and causal turn:** With no second paid shift available, can Jake enter the stoneworks social life without turning the economic pain of others into a referendum on himself? The newly signed lower table house provides a concrete independent community setting. Pell openly challenges Vessa over lost shifts; Vessa neither opens the unsafe passage nor claims certainty she does not possess; Jake testifies only to what he directly observed, not to passage safety. The economic dispute is unresolved and neither man is made a villain.
- **Materially changed state:** Jake's letter to Esi, explicitly UNSENT at Chapter XXVI close, is now handed to an independent paid carrier, Mev, whose responsibility ends at a named intermediate market. Delivery to Esi is **NOT confirmed**. Jake pays the stated carriage fee and one coin toward the table-house meal, thereby reducing—not increasing—his remaining real four-hour wages. He receives zero additional paid stoneworks hours in this chapter; the closed crossing remains closed.
- **Independent character interests:** Fara controls her eating house, its limits, and sign; the sign still does not guarantee food or specific hours. Pell needs income for real family obligations and is not compelled into forgiveness; Vessa retains safety authority without knowing when safe reopening will be possible. Eren's next-season watch decision is still unresolved and she has no interior viewpoint. Noll tends a cousin's roof and chooses to invite Jake to a social table; Mev provides carriage only within a bounded trade route. All actions are externally observed through Jake's limited POV.
- **Continuity preserved:** Chapter XXVI four paid hours and secondhand covering remain earned and purchased; previous arrival delay remains unpaid; Esi's stolen ledger leaves, probable cover, unidentified thief and private claimant records, unrelated clog, Sola's panel, Mara with Senn, Len's damaged eye and borrowed whetstone, disputed supplier debt, singer refunds, wheelwright daughter's unsigned commission, Jake's unpaid shelter pledge, Eren's undecided future and Vessa's closed passage remain unresolved. A paid letter in transit is not a delivered message or a repayment.
- **Provenance / exclusions:** The 2016 source archive does not authenticate the historical Jake owner slot. No fantasy-week chronology, ESPN scores, other owners, outcomes or real-world historical motives are asserted. The fictional lower table house, trade-carrier route, meal, household disputes and social customs require future period Character Canon and Atlas review. No Edrin/Edin or non-twelve interior POV is entered; the fictional Mev and others are supporting characters only.
- **Self-critical editorial challenges (not independent acceptance):** Pell's economic case must not be reduced to a lesson for Jake; the table house must exist for Fara and its patrons rather than as a moral classroom; Mev's route and charge need Atlas/economic plausibility review; the dinner conversation may repeat the novel's broader theme of responsibility too explicitly; ask whether the first real glimpse of off-duty social life creates sufficient dramatic variation after many work-and-ledger chapters. The source-exact reading copy and Python regression tests are mechanical only and cannot certify literary quality. No independent Authors Council, Character Canon, Atlas, Umpire or Founder approval is represented. No founder-only promotion, merge or publication occurs.

# The Sleeve Left Unstitched

*2016 Commonwealth historical causal novel — Chapter XXVIII candidate. NONCANON / INDEPENDENT EDITORIAL HOLD. Sole limited interior viewpoint: proposed fictional Jake / CHAR-JAKE-KALOPER / PRINCIPAL_CHARACTER, one of the twelve exclusive owner-principal identities. This is NOT an authenticated historical ESPN owner-slot association. All hill-country people, dye trades, houses, journeys, arrangements and conversations are invented secondary-world proposals, not evidence of actual league events or real owners' private lives. Story-time follows Chapter XXVII after Mev accepted Jake's letter for a bounded intermediate handoff; delivery to Esi remains unconfirmed. The kiln passage remains closed and Pell's lost shifts unresolved. The protected 2026 Prologue and Chapters I–III are untouched. No Character Canon, Atlas, independent Authors Council, Umpire or Founder approval is implied.*

### I. What the table could hold

By morning the benches had been turned upside down on the tables, and the little house was full of the sound of work that did not belong to a feast.

Fara had opened both windows. On one ledge stood a bowl of ashes and on the other a jar of crooked nails. A woman Jake had not seen the evening before was scraping hard lentils from the bottom of a pot. The room smelt of soapwort, onion skin, and wet wood. He had come looking for Noll, having remembered the older man's promise to help a cousin with the roof, and found instead a labor that had no place for promises.

Fara saw him through the doorway.

“If you want breakfast, there's bread in the kitchen. If you want Noll, he left for the lower houses before the light reached my sign. If you've come to ask whether your letter has arrived, I'll save you the trouble.”

“I wasn't going to ask.”

“You were going to stand looking at me until I guessed.”

Jake stepped inside. There was a piece of blue cloth on the bench nearest the hearth, rolled round something heavy. One end was fraying. Fara noticed his attention and reached across to move it farther from the cleaning water.

“Someone will come for that.”

“Did they leave it after supper?”

“After the argument, if you must know. It was here before the first bowl. People leave things before they begin to quarrel and remember them afterward as though the quarrel stole them.”

Jake took a brush and offered to clear the hearth. Fara considered him, then gave him the bucket of cold ashes to carry to the yard. She did not offer him an hour's wage, and he did not ask. There was a distinction between this house and Aren's shed. It was not an easy distinction to carry into an empty morning, but it existed.

When he returned, Pell stood in the doorway with a workman's bundle over his shoulder. He looked younger without the raised voice of the previous evening. The dry clay had been brushed from his trousers; his boots were clean enough to show their cracked seams. He stopped at the sight of the blue cloth.

“I thought I'd lost that.”

“You left it,” Fara replied.

“Same result when you're looking.”

“Not to the person whose floor it was lying on.”

Pell set his bundle down and unfolded the cloth. It held a half-finished coat: dark blue, narrow in the body, with one sleeve attached and the other hanging by a few long white stitches. The wool looked substantial, and at the collar an old piece of green lining had been used where anyone would have noticed a new repair.

Pell ran two fingers down the unstitched sleeve.

“That isn't mine yet,” he said.

“It was yours enough to carry here last night,” Fara answered.

He made no attempt to put it on. Instead he refolded it and tucked it beneath his arm. He had the embarrassed expression of a person discovered carrying something he had meant to keep private, though the coat itself was too ordinary to explain the embarrassment.

“Do you know where Vessa is?” Pell asked Jake.

“Not this morning.”

“Aren?”

“At the yard, perhaps. I haven't been up.”

“Then you've come down to waste daylight.”

“I came to see Noll.”

“That's a good way to spend it if you don't need any.”

Fara set down a wet cloth. She did not raise her voice.

“Pell, you may be cross with your wages. You may even be cross with my soup. But if you're going to talk to a man in my doorway as if he has taken something from you, make certain he has.”

Pell's jaw moved. Jake could see him search for an answer large enough to justify his temper, and find only the small one that stood in the room. He picked up his bundle.

“I'm going to the square,” he said. “There'll be the dye wagons today.”

“I know,” Fara answered.

Pell's hand remained on the latch. He looked at Jake again, not with the hard attention of the supper quarrel but with an urgency that made the room colder.

“If a woman in a blue apron asks for me, tell her I've been put on the passage. Tell her I'll be coming down after the shift.”

Fara stopped wringing the cleaning cloth. Jake looked from her to Pell.

“Who is she?”

“My mother.”

“Is that true? About the shift?”

Pell's face flushed.

“It'll be true when they open it.”

“It isn't open.”

“Then tell her I may be put on. That's all I want. I don't need you to say anything else.”

He was already moving away when Fara called his name. He did not turn. The door struck the frame twice in the wind before she caught it.

For a while neither of them spoke. The woman with the pot continued scraping. In the yard, something wooden fell with a small clatter and was set upright.

“Was that coat from her?” Jake asked.

Fara placed the cloth over the edge of the basin.

“You heard what he said. That's all the hearing you're owed.”

“What if she asks?”

“Then you can decide whether a man gets to hire you to tell his mother the wrong thing.”

Jake went to empty the next bucket, but the offered lie came with him. A false shift would last only until the woman asked where her son had worked. A gentler phrase might last longer. He thought of the many versions of Esi's address he had considered writing on his letter, each one shaped to make arrival more likely. A message could be arranged to sound more certain than its journey. That was what unsettled him: a short falsehood sometimes looked like a kindness from the side that needed it.

He left without eating the bread.

### II. The woman with the indigo hands

The market below the hill had a space for carts with wet goods, where the wheels could drip without making the packed ground treacherous for other traders. The smell told Jake when he was near it: sour water, old bark, the sharp mineral bite of dyed wool. Stretches of cloth hung from poles, some so dark that they seemed almost black and others pale enough to show the shadows of their threads.

He saw the woman before he saw Pell.

Her apron was blue, but it was not all one blue. A lighter patch crossed its middle, and the edges of the pockets were nearly purple. Her hands were stained to the wrists. She stood beside a low cart, refusing a price offered by a man who examined a roll of cloth as though looking for damage under the folds.

“You asked for the full measure,” she said. “That's the full measure.”

“There's a mark.”

“There's a mark where the dye caught the old knot. I showed it before I named the price.”

The buyer pressed the spot with his thumb.

“You could have turned that side inward.”

“I could have sold it to someone who doesn't mind that the cloth has been made by hands.”

The man laughed. She did not. He laid the roll down and chose a smaller one. She told him its cost, accepted the coins, and entered the transaction with a stub of charcoal on a wooden tally. Only after he had gone did she notice Jake watching.

“Are you buying?” she asked.

“No.”

“Then at least stand where the light falls on the work. You make a man wonder what's wrong with it.”

He moved out of the shadow. Her face was broad and weathered, the hair at her temples threaded with gray, and her eyes had the same abrupt, searching quality as Pell's. She wore a ring on a chain around her neck, not on her hand. Nothing about her suggested a woman who had come to the hill merely to be reassured by a son.

“I think I met Pell,” Jake said.

“Plenty of people have. Has he paid you what he owes?”

“He doesn't owe me anything.”

“Then you'll speak more kindly of him than some.”

She began winding a length of thread round the end of the remaining bolt. Jake considered telling her what Pell had asked. The commission lay plainly between him and his tongue.

“He was at Fara's this morning,” he said. “He said you might come looking.”

“Did he?”

“Yes.”

“And did he say where he'd be?”

Jake looked at the roll of wool. Its edges were uneven, but it had been wound with a care that made each turn hold the next.

“He asked me to say he'd been given work. I can't say that.”

The woman's fingers paused on the thread. She looked at him without surprise.

“What can you say?”

“He didn't have a paid shift yesterday. I haven't seen him on one today.”

“Are you his employer?”

“No.”

“Then why would you know whether he worked today?”

The question stripped the pleasure from his careful answer. He had told the truth and still arranged it to stand where a larger truth was missing.

“I wouldn't.”

“Good.” She tied off the thread. “I'm Ina. If you meet him again, you can tell him I've had breakfast and haven't died of wanting to know his accounts. I came to sell cloth. He is not the only reason I have a road.”

She lifted a wet fold onto the cart, where the rails had been covered with sacking. Jake took the other end before it touched the ground, then looked to her for permission. Ina nodded once and showed him how to lay the cloth without pressing a crease into the fresh dye.

“This one must travel open at the top,” she said. “If someone ties it down, the damp stays in it and the smell follows it all the way to the buyer.”

“Who taught you?”

“My aunt taught me to keep a pot from boiling dry. I learnt the rest by spoiling things I couldn't afford to spoil twice.”

She spoke without bitterness. It was a record of work, not a plea for admiration. In the next stall two young women were arguing about the shape of a heel they had been asked to mend. Ina called to one of them and settled the dispute with a short demonstration, folding her hand into the shape of the worn boot. The younger women returned to their own bench; the matter did not require Ina to continue overseeing them.

It astonished Jake, for a moment, how many conversations Ina might have had before he approached and how many she would have after he left. He was not accustomed to entering a story in which the new arrival had no need to explain herself.

“Pell thought you might believe he was on the kiln passage,” he said.

“He hopes I will.” She hooked the chain at her neck into her collar. “When he was small and was late home, he told me the river was higher than it was. He'd rather make the world dangerous than say he'd stopped to watch a wheel turn. People can keep the habit after they stop being small.”

Jake smiled in spite of himself. Ina did not return the smile.

“Don't use that against him. I didn't tell it to you for that.”

She looked past his shoulder and lifted one hand. Pell was crossing the square toward them, the blue coat folded against his chest. His stride shortened when he saw Jake standing by the cart. It shortened further when he saw the unbound cloth.

Ina did not greet him with a question about his shift.

“You're late to see the second dye lot,” she said. “The buyer took the narrow roll. There are two measures still on the cart.”

“I came for the coat.”

“I know.”

“You shouldn't have brought it.”

“It was in my room. I had the last seam to do. You said you'd come home to be fitted.”

“I was going to.”

“Before the weather turns, you said. That was a long time to make one sleeve wait.”

Pell pressed the folded coat more tightly under his arm. The white basting thread trembled against the blue wool where his thumb moved.

“I've got work,” he said.

Ina looked at him.

“Have you?” she asked.

He swallowed, glanced at Jake, and looked away. Around them trade continued, indifferent to the precise word that would decide whether a mother and son spent the morning quarreling.

“Not enough,” he said.

Ina took a short breath. She did not say she had known. She did not ask him to confess anything further.

“Then come and help me get the last lot out of the damp. You know how to keep the air between the folds.”

Pell set the coat on the cart and took the end of a length of wool. His hands knew where to go before Ina gave a direction. The two of them lifted it onto the frame and passed it back over a rail, the old sequence fitting them together without a word. Jake stepped aside. He had not been needed in the middle of it.

### III. The price of a sleeve

By the time the cloth was hung, the market's narrow shade had moved to the opposite side of the square. Ina had sold two more rolls. Pell had carried a bucket from the well, spoken to an old dyer about a broken handle, and told a pair of children that the dark liquid in the covered vat was not something to dip their fingers into. He had not spoken of the stoneworks.

Jake meant to leave. He had no business watching a family from the edge of its work. Yet Ina called him back as he passed the cart.

“Hold this a moment, if you have the hands.”

She gave him a small frame of pins and set the blue coat upon the tailboard. Pell made a sound of protest.

“He doesn't need to see that.”

“He's holding the pins, not judging your shoulders.”

Ina shook out the coat. The unfinished sleeve fell straight, long enough to cover Pell's knuckles when he tried it on. She had left the seam open from elbow to cuff.

“Stretch your arm.”

Pell obeyed with the wary expression of someone expecting to be found wanting. Ina measured the fall of the sleeve between two fingers, made a small mark with soap, then stepped back.

“You've grown since I cut it.”

“I was grown when you cut it.”

“Then you were smaller than you thought.”

He looked toward the crowd, embarrassed to be fitted in public. Ina took no notice. She took pins from Jake one at a time and fastened the cloth where the seam would lie. Only once did she ask Pell to lift his arm again.

“There,” she said. “You can take it off.”

Pell shrugged out of the coat too quickly and nearly tore a pin free. Ina caught his wrist.

“Slowly. I'm not holding you.”

He stopped. The line of his mouth changed, though Jake could not have named what the change meant.

“You made it for the work up here,” Pell said.

“I made it because the last one won't keep a wind off you. Where you wear it isn't my trade.”

“It is when you bring it here.”

“I brought it to be fitted. I have three deliveries and two orders to take downriver after this. I did not carry a coat over the ridge to make you come home.”

“Then why have you been asking how my work goes?”

“Because you send three words in a season and two of them are about wages. If you wrote about your breakfast, perhaps I'd ask what you ate.”

Pell put both hands on the cart. Around them the market had entered the slow hour when sellers begin deciding what not to carry home. A boy shook sawdust from a broom. The stalls beyond the dye rail were closing their awnings, but Ina still had work laid out in ordered piles.

“I told you I'd buy the green cloth,” Pell said.

“You did.”

“I can still buy it.”

“I sold it.”

He looked up. “You what?”

“To a schoolmistress with a good eye and a fair price. It wasn't a holy relic.”

“You said you'd put it by.”

“I did. For a while. Then a woman came who needed it and could pay for it. I told you I would keep it until the next market, and the next market came and went.”

Pell's face had the stunned vacancy of someone discovering that a promise had an ordinary date attached to it. He had spoken at the table about the cloth he meant to buy his mother. He had not imagined, Jake suspected, that the cloth might be sold rather than preserved as the shape of his ambition.

Ina pushed the second roll farther from the damp.

“I don't want you to buy back a measure of my own wool so I can admire you for it. You have needs. I have a shop that needs to live. Neither of us should pretend the other can't survive without being the subject of a promise.”

“It's not as simple as that.”

“No,” she said. “But the coat still needs a sleeve.”

The two of them looked at the blue wool. Ina took the pins from Jake and set them in their wooden case.

“There's work at my place,” she added, not looking at Pell. “Packing the winter orders. I'm not offering a son a bed and calling it employment. If you come, you'll have the same piece rate as anyone else. You can sleep in the spare room while you find another place. The arrangement ends with the orders.”

Pell's shoulders stiffened.

“You want me to come home.”

“I want someone who knows how not to crease wet cloth. I would like that someone to be you. There is a difference.”

“And if I say no?”

“I will hire another pair of hands and go on selling blue.”

He did not answer. Ina laid out the pins in two rows, counted them twice, and closed the case. She was not performing patience for him; there was simply a task she meant to finish.

Jake thought of the young man standing at Fara's table, demanding that Vessa give him back the days he had lost. Here was a road whose price he could count. It ran toward a mother who had made him a coat, and away from the hill where he had been trying to prove that he could live without her. Jake could imagine choosing either road and then making a long speech to excuse the choice.

Pell said only, “I need to walk.”

Ina nodded. She gave him the blue coat, still full of pins.

“Don't wear that until I've fixed it. You can bring it back, or leave it at Fara's. I won't chase you with a needle.”

Pell rolled the garment carefully, no longer attempting to hide the loose sleeve, and left by the path to the upper yards. Jake watched him go. Ina returned to her buyers. The woman who had offered him a choice did not stop working to observe whether he was grateful for it.

### IV. The commission at the open window

Jake found Pell that afternoon at the table house. Fara was sorting a heap of old crockery on a blanket in the yard, keeping the sound of broken edges away from the dining room. One cup had a handle mended with wire. Another was good only for holding a few sprigs of mint, and Fara set it aside for that purpose.

Eren sat at the open window with a narrow board across her knees. She was painting a series of small colored bars along its edge, stopping frequently to wipe her brush with a rag. On the floor beside her lay an order wrapped in plain paper. Jake could see Ina's name written across it.

Pell stood near the back wall, the blue coat resting on a chair.

Eren noticed Jake looking at the paper.

“The dye woman wants the edges of her cart boards marked,” she said. “She keeps losing the marks when the cloth is wet. I've given her a price. She has given me half.”

“Will you paint them here?”

“If she leaves the boards and Fara doesn't mind the smell.”

Fara, examining a cracked bowl, replied without lifting her head. “I mind everything that makes a smell. I tolerate some of it.”

Eren laughed, and went on painting. It was a small commission, the sort that would be invisible on the walls of a grand house and entirely useful to a woman who drove a damp cart in changing light. Eren had chosen the colors to remain visible when splashed. The painted sign above Fara's own door had brought her another buyer, and the new work would pay for brushes she had not yet acquired.

“Vessa know you're doing that?” Pell asked.

“She knows I am not on the passage.”

“She wanted you to watch the crossing.”

“She asked whether I could help on a day they measure it. There isn't a day yet. And I'm here.”

Pell looked away. Eren did not follow his glance. Her brush kept moving, and the orange line grew to the width of a grain.

Jake sat on a stool by the door. He had come because he thought the argument between Pell and his mother might have followed the coat here. He had not expected the room to contain work that had nothing to do with the quarrel. Fara's broken cups had their own histories. Eren's half-paid commission lay under a pebble so the paper would not lift. Someone had begun a kettle. Out on the street a child was practicing a tune on a reed pipe and stopping each time the same high note failed.

Pell approached the window.

“My mother hired you?”

“Yes.”

“She didn't ask me.”

“Why would she?”

The words were not sharp. Their simplicity stung him worse than any accusation. Pell lifted the coat from the chair and set it down again.

“She could've let me paint the marks.”

Eren looked at his hands. “Can you make the same line on four boards in a wet morning, with the edges the right length and the ends square?”

“I can learn.”

“Of course you can. But she wanted them finished before her cart goes downriver. She paid me for knowing how.”

Pell watched the brush again. The paint's edge was unwavering; Eren had done the difficult part often enough that it looked easy. He lowered himself to a chair opposite her. For several minutes neither spoke.

When Fara finished separating the cups, she returned to find Jake and Pell still sitting near Eren's work. She put one whole plate on the table between them with a crust of yesterday's bread.

“I didn't set a court in here,” she said. “If you're keeping company, you may help clean a shelf.”

They rose together. Fara sent Jake to the high shelf and Pell to the low one, not because their heights made it sensible but because the low shelf needed a hand steady enough to remove the crooked nails hammered into it. Pell had worked with nails before. He loosened them with the back of a small blade and arranged them carefully in a jar. Jake cleared dust and old cups from above, holding the unbroken ones in both hands.

The work passed without much talk. When the shelf was empty, Fara took the jar from Pell and nodded toward the blue coat.

“Your mother said she'd come to sew it here before supper if you don't carry it down.”

“She said that?”

“She asked whether the window had good light. I told her it did when there wasn't somebody sitting in it painting.”

Eren lifted her board a little higher.

“I can move.”

“Finish,” Fara said. “She has a needle and more than one afternoon.”

Pell's hand moved to the coat, then stopped.

“I need to decide something,” he said.

“You have all afternoon,” Fara replied. “But if the decision is whether I feed you, the answer is that I have no supper made yet.”

He did not smile. Instead he picked up the coat and went out to the yard. The pipe in the street faltered again on the same high note. Jake wanted to follow him. He stayed where he was, carrying a stack of cups back to the shelf. He had nothing to say that would make Pell's choice less his own.

### V. The road without a witness

Ina came as the light began to leave the windows. She entered with the short, businesslike step Jake had seen at the dye cart, but her sleeves were rolled down and there was a smear of charcoal on her cheek. Eren had finished the first board and was showing Fara the two colors together. The others were sitting at the nearest table with bread, pickled roots and the last of the soft cheese.

Pell was not there.

Ina's eyes went to the chair where he had left the coat, now folded with the unstitched sleeve on top. She placed her sewing case beside it and took out a needle.

“Has he gone?” she asked.

“He went to the upper path,” Fara answered. “He didn't say where after.”

Ina sat by the window, threaded the needle, and looked at the coat without beginning to sew. She could finish the seam without Pell, but the pins were still in their places and his arm had been measured while he was angry. A finished sleeve made to an angry measurement might not fit by morning.

“He asked me to tell you he'd been given work,” Jake said.

Ina glanced at him.

“You told me that already.”

“I know. I thought perhaps—”

“He can tell me what he meant when he has decided. I'm here to mend the cloth, not to decide for him.”

Outside, the pipe found its high note. It was thin and not beautiful, but the child held it without breaking until breath ran out. The sound moved along the street, then came back as another attempt at the tune.

Eren packed her brushes. She had paid for the room she used with the work she would finish for Ina, and Fara would receive a little painted board of her own in exchange for letting the workshop remain open through the afternoon. Nothing grand was being founded. A painter had found a place to work with light, and a trader had found a painter who kept her measure.

Pell returned after dusk, while Fara was clearing the last crumbs. He carried his workman's bundle on his back. Jake noticed that it had been retied, the straps pulled evenly, and the little metal cup that had hung loose in the morning now secured within the roll.

“I went to Aren,” Pell said to Ina.

“And?”

“I told him I can't wait for the passage. He said there may be work in the sheds, but not enough for what I need.”

Ina set down the needle. She neither applauded nor reproached him.

“I asked about another yard too,” he went on. “They offered hauling for three days. After that I don't know.”

“Do you want the three days?”

“I do. I can sleep at the yard if the weather holds. When it's done I may come down to help pack your orders, if the work is still there.”

“It may be.”

“I know.”

“And if I have hired someone?”

“Then I'll look for another place.”

For the first time since the table-house quarrel, Pell spoke without asking someone else to make his uncertainty disappear. Jake had no idea whether the three days would go well. There was nothing in the young man's face that made a happy outcome certain.

Ina beckoned him nearer. “Put the coat on again.”

Pell set down the bundle and obeyed. She drew the sleeve carefully over his arm, found the soap mark, and removed two pins.

“It fits,” she said. “If you want it finished, I can do the rest while you eat.”

“I thought you were going downriver tonight.”

“Tomorrow, when the last cloth dries.”

“I could've come after the hauling.”

“You could,” she said. “Or you could have the coat before you go. I made it for your shoulders, not for a bargain.”

He sat. She sewed in the clear circle of lamplight beside the window while Fara found him a bowl. The other diners had left. Eren carried her finished board to the wall where it would dry without being touched. Jake watched the needle go in, come out, and vanish through wool that had seemed too thick to pierce. Neither Ina nor Pell said what had been decided between them, because the decision was not a single thing.

When the last seam was turned, Ina gave Pell the coat. She did not ask him to promise to return. He did not offer a future purchase of green wool as proof that he loved her. He stood, put it on, and bent his arms. The sleeves reached his wrists.

“It's warm,” he said.

“That's the purpose of a coat.”

He kissed her cheek so quickly that Jake almost missed it. Ina put her needle back into its case. Then Pell went out with his bundle toward the upper road, where the hauling yard had been offered for three uncertain days.

For a while Ina remained at the table with Fara. They spoke of a buyer who had demanded the same blue from two separate dye pots, of a wheel with an uneven rim, of the price of good thread. Pell's name did not disappear from their conversation because he had gone. It simply ceased to be the only thing they had to talk about.

Jake rose to leave. At the door he found Eren wiping the final trace of paint from a brush. She glanced toward the path Pell had taken.

“Will he come back?” he asked.

“I don't know,” she said. “I hope his coat fits when he does.”

She turned the brush between her fingers and inspected its bristles. A painter's concern, not a prophecy.

Jake went up the hill. Vessa's pale warning strip was visible in the dark against the sealed crossing. The stoneworks had not reopened. No additional paid hours had been offered him that day. His letter was still in Mev's bounded journey, with no proof of delivery to Esi. The shelter owed him nothing for telling a difficult truth at a dye cart. Ina owed him nothing for having witnessed the fitting. Pell's choice did not redeem Jake's own unsettled obligations, and the coat was not a reward meant for him to understand.

In his sleeping room the space beside his pallet was empty. The cup balanced on the neighboring shelf had been moved; someone had cleaned beneath it. Jake took the few coins left from his four paid hours and counted them once. The price of a letter, a meal and the bedding had made the number smaller than he wished. He did not turn the coins into a sentence about thrift. He placed them in his bag and lay down.

From farther below the ridge came the faint unfinished tune of the child with the reed pipe. It found the difficult note, lost it, and found it again. He could hear no word of the song.

---

### Editorial handoff — noncanonical candidate, not an approval

- **Chapter job / question:** Move the hill's social world beyond Jake's own employment and witness Pell and his independently employed mother negotiate a future neither can guarantee. Can Pell stop protecting a fiction of wages long enough to choose a concrete, temporary alternative without surrendering his independence?
- **Sole owner POV:** Proposed fictional CHAR-JAKE-KALOPER / PRINCIPAL_CHARACTER; limited interior access only to Jake. Supporting Fara, Pell, Ina, Eren and Vessa receive dialogue, independent objectives and observable acts, never interior narration. 2016 historical owner-slot binding remains unverified.
- **Entering state:** Pell's lost wages remain real; Vessa's kiln passage is closed; Jake has four paid stoneworks hours total, a paid letter carried only to an intermediary, and unfulfilled shelter obligations; Eren completed Fara's sign and has not accepted Vessa's future passage-watch duty.
- **Turn / material action:** Pell asks Jake to misstate his employment to Ina; Jake offers only bounded observation. Ina demonstrates that her dye trade exists independently of Pell, offers him piece-rate work rather than sentimental rescue, and continues her business. Pell obtains a separate three-day hauling offer rather than a guaranteed season. Ina fits and finishes the previously uncompleted sleeve. Eren secures an actual separate painting commission from Ina.
- **Exit state / continuing loops:** Pell leaves for an uncertain three-day hauling job, without an assured longer position; Ina has independent commercial deliveries and no promise of reunion; Eren's cart-mark order is partially paid and not yet all delivered; kiln still closed, Jake earns zero additional paid stoneworks hours, his letter to Esi has no confirmed delivery, and all prior shelter debts, stolen-ledger questions, Mara/Senn, Sola's screen and related unresolved threads persist. No new external world canon is established.
- **Omitted source events:** No 2016 week, ESPN matchup, opponent, score, result, trade, championship, historical owner pairing or real-life motive is placed in fiction because the relevant historical owner binding and supporting evidence are absent. This chapter does not assert an actual seasonal weather record, market geography or period employment practice.
- **Editorial risks / independent hold:** Five movements risk repeating the novel's debt-and-honesty concerns despite the new family and craft relationships. Ina's business competence should not become an uncomplicated ideal; Pell's separate hauling offer must remain economically uncertain; Jake's vantage should not usurp their agency. Textile dyeing, garments, trade routes, cart-mark painting, housing and settlement timing require historical craft and Atlas review.
